# Traceability

Traces every requirement from design through to a passing test and the release it
shipped in. Part of the Definition of Done in `CLAUDE.md`.

The roadmap (§37) provides a **family-level** baseline proving every requirement
family has an implementation route. **Stage 2 expands this to one row per
individual requirement** (`M##-FR-##`, `D##-FR-##`, `SEC-##`, `PRV-##`, `AI-NFR-##`,
`MG-##`, etc.) with design, code, automated-test and release references. No
requirement may reach **Done** without a complete individual row.

## Family-level baseline (roadmap §37)

**The Status column is derived from the requirement rows below it, never asserted here.** This table
sat at *"Not started"* on all twelve families through nineteen stages of work, because the
integrity guardrail matched only rows beginning `| M##-FR-## |` and never saw these. It is now
checked: `tests/guardrails/traceability-integrity.test.ts` recomputes each family from its own
requirement rows and fails if a family claims anything else.

| Requirement family | Workflow | Screens | Contract | Data | Test family | Release | Status |

> **"Built" here means the domain logic and its tests, not a deployed system.** The apps, the
> service layer and the store edge are assembled to differing degrees — see *Assembly state*
> below, which is the honest counterpart to this table and the reason it must be read beside it.

| POS service · API-05 (§31.1, hard rules #1/#2/#10, P-08, M12–M15) | `services/pos/` (`sale-intake`, `index`) | **The server does not get to say no.** By the time a sale reaches this endpoint it has already happened — the till committed locally (#1), the money is in the drawer, the receipt is printed, the customer has gone. A server that refuses a sale it dislikes and a till that treats refusal as *"drop it"* together delete a sale that really happened while the cash for it sits in the drawer, and the day will not balance with the evidence thrown away by both systems that had it. So **a well-formed sale is always banked and everything wrong with it becomes a visible exception attached to it** (#10, P-08); `banked` is typed as the literal `true`, and the reply is **202, never 4xx**. The only refusal is a payload that cannot be *read* as a sale, and it tells the lane to keep it rather than discard it. **The server never corrects a sale to make it valid** — the customer paid what the till charged, and rewriting it to the catalogue price makes the books agree with the catalogue and disagree with reality (#2, proven by scanning the module's exports). Findings are ranked by how fast a person must act rather than by value: **a recalled product sold outranks every number on the list**, because it is in a customer's hands, and the lane is not blamed for it (its pack predated the block). A price difference from a lane that was behind is *informational* — pricing from the pack it holds is exactly what a lane is supposed to do offline — and *material* only when the lane was current. Late is not wrong; a clock ahead of the server is. Informational findings are **counted, not listed**, because forty explained differences from one lane back off a long weekend bury the one that is not explained. Tested in `tests/unit/service-pos.test.ts` |
| **Thirteen APIs, one surface** (§30, SEC-03, §31.1) | `tests/integration/thirteen-apis-one-surface.test.ts` | The questions that only exist once all thirteen are assembled and that no single service's tests can reach: every API in the catalogue is actually served and none is invented; no two services claim one address; **every write on the whole surface is idempotent**, because the till, the handheld and the driver's phone all resend what they could not confirm; every path is versioned and every route declares a permission; and **each permission is spelled one way across every service** — `pos.sale.read` against `pos.sales.read` both look right in review and one of them guards an endpoint with a permission nobody holds, so case, separator *and plural* are normalised away, with a tripwire proving the normalisation catches the near-miss. The API catalogue is **read from `docs/api/catalogue.md`** rather than restated, so the two cannot drift. 12 assertions |
| Catalogue service · API-02 (§31, M03/M05, §28, M10-FR-04, P-01, P-08) | `services/catalogue/` (`pack`, `index`) | The first domain service on the kernel, and **the file the shop trades on when the internet is down**. A bad read shows one person a wrong answer; a bad **pack** installs on every lane and is the only truth any of them has — and the lane cannot ring anybody about it, because not being able to is why it holds a pack. Refused before anything is signed: **a pack much smaller than the shop** (the truncated-export failure pointed outward, and worse — it is internally consistent, signs, installs, and the first to notice is a customer whose item will not scan; a real discontinuation is **acknowledged by count**, and an acknowledgement that does not match what left is refused as a rubber stamp); a version that does not move forward (a price rollback nobody performed); prices approved by whoever drafted them (§28 surviving the step that reaches the shelf edge); a product with no tax class (OC-21); a price above MRP (a lane would charge it offline all week with nobody able to stop it). On the lane: **a pack that fails its signature is rejected and the shop keeps trading on the last one it trusted** — `shopKeepsTrading: true`, never a blank catalogue, never a stopped till, and never silently (staleness is shown in days). HMAC-SHA256 with `timingSafeEqual`; the signing key comes from the environment (#4). Found by building it: the kernel's card-data guard fired on **EAN-13 barcodes**, whose check digit is computed the same alternating way Luhn is — see the kernel row. Tested in `tests/unit/service-catalogue.test.ts` |
| Service kernel (§30 conventions, §31.1, §27.1, SEC-02/03/07/12, OB-01) | `services/kernel/` (`errors`, `router`, `pipeline`) | The foundation all thirteen APIs sit on, so no cross-cutting rule is written thirteen times and correct twelve. **Every convention is enforced at route REGISTRATION, not at request time** — a service serving an unversioned path, a write with no idempotency, or an endpoint with no permission **fails to start**, rather than failing on the one request in a thousand that exercises the gap; `buildRouter` names every malformed route at once. **The three-part error (§27.1) cannot be constructed incomplete** — and the part every API omits, *whether the data was saved*, has no default, with `unknown` a legitimate answer for a timeout, because answering "failed" when the truth is unknown is what takes the money twice. **Two guards run on the way OUT and refuse rather than redact:** a reply carrying another tenant's id is not sent (the backstop for the one query that was not scoped), and a reply carrying something structurally a card number is not sent (hard rule #3) — Luhn-checked **plus an issuer prefix plus a card-length check**, because Luhn alone was not enough: EAN-13's check digit is computed the same alternating way, so about **one barcode in ten passes Luhn by chance** — the first version of the guard blocked a catalogue pack, the single most important response in the system. Lengths 13 and 14 are excluded deliberately (EAN-13, ITF-14), which costs a legacy 13-digit Visa and buys a guard quiet enough to leave switched on. A different request under a used `Idempotency-Key` is **refused, never answered with the stored result** — a sale of 400 under the key of a sale of 250 would otherwise report success and bank 250. Found and fixed while building: the audit fired only on the success path, so a **refused** write left no trace, which is the event an audit trail exists for. Tested in `tests/unit/service-kernel.test.ts` |

| Store edge (P-01, #1, NFR-03, §31) | `edge/store-edge/` (`durability`, `index`) | **The receipt prints after the durable write, never before.** The other order produces the worst failure the product has: a sale the cashier saw succeed, the customer paid for and walked away from, which was never written anywhere — nobody finds out, the day is short, and the till is blamed for a counting error that never happened. It is also **the one place in the product where refusing a sale is correct**, and the reason is the moment: the edge refuses *before* the sale completes (nothing has happened, the customer is still standing there), while `services/pos` refuses *after* (the money is in the drawer and they have gone). Same word, opposite consequences. When the disk fills, `SHED_ORDER` decides what is given up — telemetry, prefetched catalogue, synced history, then pending movements, which a recount can rebuild — and **an unsynced sale is last, with nothing permitted after it**; `shedsAnySale` is typed as the literal `false`, and where shedding everything else is not enough the lane refuses new sales rather than deleting money already taken. Health is reported in **hours behind and hours of headroom**, not items pending, because "247 items" is a number staff learn to ignore. Tested in `tests/unit/store-edge.test.ts` |
| API client (§30, §31.1, §27.1, P-01, P-08) | `packages/api-client/` | **The idempotency key is minted when the person acts, not when the request is sent** — the reason this package exists. Generate it per attempt and a retry carries a new one, so a till resending a sale it could not confirm creates a second sale, and the server doing everything right banks both. Every server-side guarantee is undone by a client that gets this wrong, and it is wrong by default in almost every HTTP client, because retrying is a transport concern and the key looks like a transport detail. An `Intent` is created once and carries its key through every attempt, reconnection and restart — proved by a test that restarts the application and checks the key survived. **`wasItSaved` reaches the person** in words rather than being flattened to "request failed". Offline writes queue and say nothing is lost (P-01); a decided refusal is *not* retried, because resending it forever buries everything behind it; the queue drains oldest-first, since a refund arriving before its sale reaches a server that has never heard of the sale. Reads fall back to cache **with their age in the answer**, telling the reader to stop deciding on figures once they are hours old. Tested in `tests/unit/api-client.test.ts` |
| Persistence adapters (hard rule #2, M08, §29, OB-01) | `services/api/src/adapters.ts` | Until these, the API booted, answered and **forgot everything** — every service was wired to a stub. **Everything is an event**: there is no products table, no sales table, no stock table and no "current pack" column. A sale is `SaleCommitted`, a movement is `InventoryMoved`, a published price list is `CataloguePublished`, and every balance and every *current* anything is **projected by reading the stream forward**. That is not taste — it is the only shape in which hard rule #2 can hold, because a table with an `UPDATE` on it is a quantity somebody can overwrite and no discipline in the application layer survives one hurried fix. A test asserts no mutable `sales`, `products`, `stock` or `inventory` table exists. Sale dedupe is keyed on the **sale's own id**, so a till resending collapses to one even under a different transport key. Proved end to end in `tests/integration/it-remembers.test.ts` against real PostgreSQL: publish a pack, sell against it, resend twice, project stock from out-of-order movements, and watch the database refuse an `UPDATE`. Three faults found by running it — an append that **deduped against a previous test run's pack** (the store right, the test wrong, now run-scoped), a query string put in the path instead of the query field, and a fixture dated ahead of the real clock which `committed_in_the_future` caught doing its job |
| An empty answer is not an all-clear (hard rule #2, QG-07, M06, M20, P-08) | `services/api/src/adapters.ts`, `services/purchase/src/index.ts`, `services/finance/src/index.ts`, `services/fulfilment/src/index.ts` | Backing the remaining five services with a real store exposed **four defects that had nothing to do with persistence** and would have shipped without it. Three of the new projections fold streams nothing writes to yet — purchase orders, the chart of accounts, the dispatch list — and for each one the empty answer was an *all-clear*: `threeWayMatch` over no lines returned `blocked: false`, "invoiced 0, matched", and told the owner *the order, the delivery and the invoice agree*; `closePeriod` over no control total was **vacuously satisfied** and closed the month on a signature, reporting "0 control total(s) agreed" — a sentence that reads like success when it means nothing was checked; `reconcileRun` checked only assigned-minus-attempted, so with an empty dispatch list a driver could return five deliveries and a bag of cash and the run reported *the run reconciles and every order has an outcome*; and open commitments returned `{count: 0}`, which an owner reads as **we have nothing on order** and buys against. The system was most confident exactly where it knew least. Fixed in the **domains**, not papered over in the adapters: no lines is now blocked *because nothing was compared*, and the wording distinguishes checked-and-clean from not-checked, since only one is a reason to pay; `nothing_was_checked` refuses before the signature is even considered, because otherwise the first thing a person hears is "sign it" and signing is what they can do; `unassigned` reports the mirror case — a delivery nobody dispatched, which has money attached; commitments answer **not known**, following the loyalty-points precedent, because a zero and an unknown are different answers. **Not one existing test failed when the four guards were added** — which is the finding: no test exercised the empty case, which is precisely why all four were wrong. Also found here: a supplier bank change carried **no request date**, so a supplier moving to a new account and later moving back had the return collapse into the original change as a replay, leaving the ledger asserting the money still goes to the middle account — `requestedAt` is now required and refused first; and `JournalPosted` was stamped with the **document's own date** rather than the time we recorded it, which would have put the ledger's clock under the control of whoever typed the date on the paper. `controlTotals` honestly returns nothing and the close refuses: every figure this API holds for a period comes down one path, so adding two of them up and comparing is one figure written twice — the genuine second source is the bank statement, the filed return or the counted shelf, and until one is fed in a month that closes is a month nobody checked. 25 new assertions across `tests/unit/service-commerce.test.ts`, `tests/unit/service-customer-fulfilment.test.ts` and `tests/integration/it-remembers.test.ts`, each with a tripwire proving the guard does not simply refuse everything |
| A token becomes a principal (API-01, SEC-02/03, M02-FR-01, OB-01, hard rules #3 #4) | `services/identity/src/token.ts`, `services/kernel/src/config.ts`, `services/api/src/main.ts` | `authenticate` returned `undefined` for every caller until this — default-deny rather than a bypass, so nothing was exposed, but nobody could use the system either. **It verifies, and it cannot issue.** No password, passkey or MFA secret is held anywhere: that is the deployment identity provider's job, and M02-FR-01 is a deliberate partial precisely so this codebase never holds one. A test scans the module's exports to prove there is no `sign`, `issue` or `mint` — a module that can make tokens is a module whose secret is a token factory. Every check is shaped around a real, repeatedly-shipped bug, and every one of them reduces to **believing something the token said about itself**: `alg: none` and the RS256→HS256 confusion attack are both "the header chose the algorithm", so the algorithm comes from configuration and the header is *checked against* it rather than consulted for it; **the signature is verified before a single claim is read**, because until then every byte of the payload is text an attacker wrote — proved by a token that is both badly signed and long expired, which must fail on the signature, since reaching the expiry check at all means the payload was parsed and trusted first; `exp` is **required rather than defaulted**, because a token that never expires still works after the person who held it has left; `iss` and `aud` are pinned, because a correctly signed unexpired token from a different system, or for a different service of ours, is still not a token for here — and the audience check does not degrade to "some audience was present" when `aud` is a list. **`tenantId` comes from the signed payload and nowhere else** (OB-01): there is no header, query parameter or option by which a caller can supply one. The caller is told "unauthenticated" and **never the reason** — "the signature did not verify" and "that token expired" are different sentences and the difference is free information for whoever is trying tokens — while the reason goes to the operator's log, which never contains any part of the token. The three IdP settings are **required, not optional**: absent, `authenticate` would have to fall back to something and every fallback there is a way in, so a deployment with no identity provider **does not start**, which is louder than one that starts and lets everybody in. CI proves the refusal by name. Found here by the guardrail that reads `.env.example` from disk: the placeholder check matched **exact strings**, so the newly added `REPLACE_WITH_YOUR_IDENTITY_PROVIDER_URL` sailed through and a deployment would have believed tokens from an issuer literally called that — it now matches the `REPLACE_WITH` prefix, with a tripwire proving it fires on an invented placeholder and still passes a generated key. 22 assertions in `tests/unit/identity-token.test.ts` |
| All thirteen persist — and the same fault three more times (API-01/10/11/12/13, M02, SEC-03, AID-01…10, hard rules #5 #6) | `services/api/src/adapters.ts`, `services/api/src/roles.ts`, `services/platform/src/index.ts`, `services/reporting/src/index.ts`, `services/migration/src/index.ts` | Identity, platform, reporting, migration and AI now read and write the event store, so **every one of the thirteen services persists**. The same empty-answer fault appeared three more times and was fixed in the domains each time. **`assessHealth([])` returned healthy** — "all 0 dependencies reachable" — because `filter` over nothing finds nothing and nothing found reads as nothing wrong; a monitoring gap and a working system are not the same state, and this is the module whose entire point is that the process being alive is not the same as it working, so green-because-unchecked was that fault one level up. It now answers **`unknown`**, deliberately not `unhealthy`, which means the shop cannot trade and would trigger a failover for what is a gap in monitoring — the same three-way shape as the kernel's `saved / not_saved / unknown`. **`dashboard([])` said "0 figures, all current"** — `reduce` seeded with `live` over no figures returns `live` — which is the sentence a person reads as everything being fine; an empty dashboard is not a clean one. And the migration service was handed **`'u-owner'` and `'u-operator'` as literal strings**: the control *only the owner may accept a figure into the opening books* was satisfied by anybody who typed that string, and the page the owner and the CA sign named a person who does not exist — worse than a page that will not render, because it is signed, and the rule that whoever ran the extraction cannot choose which stock lines get counted only means something if the page says who that was. Both are now `string \| undefined`, the owner is **read from who actually holds the owner role**, and the routes refuse — naming *both* gaps at once — rather than proceeding on a placeholder. `services/api/src/roles.ts` holds the catalogue in the composition root because **a role is configuration and its holders are tenant data**: what `cashier` *means* is a set of permission codes this product defines, and separation of duties is not a policy document but which codes are absent from a list — the accountant can post a journal and cannot close the period they posted into, the store manager runs the shop and cannot grant a role. **The AI kill switch defaults ON**, the one place in the adapters where an absent record does not mean "we cannot say": a switch that defaults off is an agent running because nobody has told it not to, and no budget granted means nothing may be spent (hard rule #5). Health probes are a **real call every request**, not a cached flag — a cached `reachable: true` reports the last time things were fine. 17 new assertions across `tests/unit/service-store-core.test.ts`, `tests/unit/service-commerce.test.ts`, `tests/unit/service-governance.test.ts` and `tests/integration/it-remembers.test.ts`, each guard with a tripwire |
| The customer's shopping session (M16, D06, D08, P-02, hard rule #3) | `apps/customer-app/src/shopping-session.ts` | The rules already live in `packages/storefront`; what this holds is the thing no single function can — **the order of events**, and what the customer is truthfully told at each point. **An order is not placed until the shop has it.** On a phone with no signal the basket is prepared and nothing else: the screen says *not sent yet*, never *order placed* — and there is no `markPlaced` or `assumeSent` anywhere in the module, proved by scanning its exports, because the moment one exists the offline path acquires a shortcut somebody takes to make a screen look tidier. That is the deliberate **inverse of the till** (hard rule #1) and the difference is worth stating because getting it backwards is easy: at the till we commit locally first because the money is already in the drawer and the customer has walked out — the event happened and refusing to record it loses it — whereas on a phone nothing has happened at all, no money moved, no goods left, and the shop has never heard of this basket. Three more, all about sequence: **nothing is paid for that has not been reviewed**, because `reviewCart` exists so a customer hears at review rather than at the payment screen or the door, and a session that lets checkout skip it makes that function optional; **a review goes stale** — the pack version is carried with the review and a basket reviewed on pack 7 will not be charged while the shop is on pack 8, since that bills a figure the customer never saw (P-02), and they are sent back to look rather than quietly repriced; and **a short line is reduced only when the customer says so**, never silently, because a basket that quietly becomes a smaller basket is a customer who finds out at the door. A payment reference shaped like a card number is **refused, not redacted** — redacting means it was held first, and by then it is in memory, in a crash report and in whatever the phone wrote to disk (hard rule #3) — with a tripwire proving a provider token of the same length still goes through. A pending payment is reported as **waiting**, never as done: confirming against a payment that may not exist is how a shop picks, packs and delivers goods it was never paid for and finds out at settlement. 18 assertions in `tests/unit/customer-app.test.ts` |
| The cutover weekend (MG-01…MG-12, QG-11, hard rules #6 #7, P-01, P-08) | `docs/runbooks/cutover-weekend.md` | The hour-by-hour plan for the weekend the shop changes system, and **every decision on it is taken now, while nobody is tired**. Two things shape the rest: **the point of no return is late on purpose** — right up to Monday's opening the answer to "should we go back?" is *yes, and it costs us a weekend*, so the decision to open is taken **on Monday at 06:00 with the checks in front of you**, not on Saturday evening when the load has just finished and everybody is pleased with themselves — and **the old system is never switched off**, only made read-only and kept running ninety days, because that costs nothing and is the only thing that makes "go back" a real option rather than a thing we say. Friday freezes and copies before anything reads (MG-02); Saturday extracts and refuses a short file by name; Saturday evening is the first Go/Stop; Sunday counts value-weighted, runs the six outside-evidence checks and holds a **dress rehearsal with the internet physically unplugged**, which is the one that decides whether there is a product at all (P-01); Monday morning asks four questions of which any *no* is a Stop. The **stop conditions are tabulated and absolute** — no judgement calls at 2am — and include one that is easy to miss: the latest rehearsal's exception list being **longer** than the previous one, which means something is getting worse and we do not yet know what. The shift-in-charge's refusal to open outranks everybody's schedule, because they are the ones who will be standing there. Costs are stated both ways: going back costs a weekend, going forward on any stop condition costs the trading week and the customers who could not be served |
| The cost of a sale does not grow with the shop's history (NFR-03, §32, hard rule #1, M08) | `tests/performance/api-scales-with-the-shop.test.ts`, `services/pos/src/index.ts`, `services/inventory/src/index.ts`, `packages/persistence/src/event-store.ts`, `db/migrations/0006_stream_type_index.sql` | **The defect was in a type, which is why reading the code did not reveal it.** `PosDeps` said `bankedSaleIds: (tenantId) => Set<string>` and `receiptNumbers: (tenantId) => Map<string, string>` — and `acceptSale` took **one lookup from each**. A port that hands back the whole history obliges every implementation to read the whole history, so no adapter written against it could have been quick, and the cost was paid **on every sale**. The arithmetic is what makes it serious rather than untidy: SRE takes ~2,000 sales a day, which is 700,000 events in a year, so by month three each till was waiting on a scan of a quarter of a million rows to be told a two-word answer. It would not have shown up against a hundred fixtures, would not have shown up in the pilot's first week, and would have arrived as *"the tills have got slow"* with nothing obviously changed. The ports now **ask the question** — `isBanked(tenantId, saleId)`, `saleHoldingReceipt(tenantId, receiptNumber)`, `isKnown(tenantId, movementId)` — and the adapters answer each with one hit on `(tenant_id, idempotency_key)`, an index the store already had to carry because that uniqueness is what makes `append` idempotent. A **receipt-number index entry** is appended alongside each sale and is deliberately allowed to lose the race: a second sale claiming a taken number finds the key held and the entry keeps naming the **first** sale, which is exactly what the exception says — the index records who holds the number, not who asked last. Two more found by the test rather than by reading: `latest()` read a whole stream to take the end of it, which for a shop publishing a price list a day meant deserialising 365 full product masters to answer "which version are we on?" — on every sale, since the POS route checks the pack version before banking — so the store gained `latestOfType` with a matching index (migration 0006, additive: an index and nothing else); and `InMemoryEventStore.readStream` **filtered every record it held**, so reading a three-event stream cost the same as reading a three-hundred-thousand-event one, and the reference implementation had quietly stopped modelling the contract it exists to define — which matters beyond tests, because that is the store the edge runs on. The tests are **ratios rather than absolute times**, so they mean the same thing on a laptop, in CI and on the shop's back-office PC, and one of them checks the **whole** intake read path rather than a single call, because one constant-time lookup next to a scan is still a scan. The harness takes the **best of three rounds** rather than one, which *tightens* the test: a round can only be made slower by something outside the code under test, so the fastest is the closest reading of the work itself. When one of these flaked under full-suite load, widening the ratio was the wrong repair for the reason the whole suite exists — a test loose enough never to flake is a test that cannot fail. The noise was real and the measurement was too small to see past it, so the answer was a better measurement. **And the same again for stock, where it is unbounded rather than merely large.** A hypermarket generates a few thousand movements a day — well over a million a year — and every availability lookup replayed all of them, which is somebody standing in an aisle or a customer watching a page. `project` now accepts an **opening balance** and the adapter folds from the last `InventorySnapshotTaken`, an event written on the *write* path (a handheld can afford an occasional extra fold; a lookup cannot) and keyed on the sequence it covers, so two writers racing produce one snapshot rather than two that disagree about where they start. **A snapshot is not a second source of truth**: it is derived from the ledger, it is itself append-only, and it can be deleted and rebuilt — an integration test proves a projection read *through* a snapshot equals a fold of the raw ledger, and the click-and-collect promise then reads through that same snapshot, so drift would surface as a wrong promise to a customer rather than as an abstract assertion. The first version of the snapshot work **passed its timing test while still reading every event and filtering to the tail in JavaScript** — correct, bounded in fold cost, unbounded in the read that actually reaches the database, and invisible on a stopwatch because filtering a million rows is quick compared with folding them. `readStream` gained `sinceSeq` and `type`, narrowed **at the store** (a range scan on `event_ledger_stream_idx`, and a binary search rather than a filter in the in-memory reference, since that is the store the edge runs on), and the guard for it **counts rows rather than milliseconds** — with a tripwire proving the counter does see a full read, so the assertion is not vacuous. **Two more of the same class, both in code written the same day.** *Today's takings* read every sale ever banked and then filtered to today — the one query in the system guaranteed to run against the largest table every morning, forever, because the owner looks at it daily; `readStream` gained a `from`/`to` window on `occurredAt`, served by `event_ledger_type_idx`, and the trading-day check stays *on top of* the window rather than replacing it, because the window is what makes the read cheap and the trading day is what makes it right when the shop trades past midnight (M09). And **consent was one stream for the whole shop**, so answering *"may we text this customer?"* — with somebody waiting at the counter — read every consent record the tenant held, a hundred thousand rows at twenty thousand loyalty customers; it is now **one stream per customer**, which is the shape the store's `(tenant_id, stream, seq)` index was built for — and the same correction was then applied across the surface: **a stream per driver-run** (settling one driver's Tuesday no longer reads every delivery the shop has ever made, and the two filters that used to do it are now the stream's name), **per location** for reservations, **per invoice** for purchase lines; while six reads that folded a whole stream to look at its last item — the AI kill switch, the budget cap, the enabled agents, the extraction operator — became one indexed row each. **The systematic guard is the one that will still be here for the fourteenth adapter**: it seeds a shop with twenty thousand events none of the reads care about, makes **every** adapter read, counts what came back, and names every one over budget **at once** — because one per run is several rounds of the same discovery. It found a case the individual tests had missed, and the finding was the **fixture**: it dated all ten thousand sales today, so the dashboard's per-day bound looked like no bound at all. A fixture that compresses a year into one day cannot show the difference between reading a day and reading a year, and the sales are now spread across days at the rate a shop actually trades. 12 assertions |
| The shop actually reaches the cloud (§31, §31.1, P-01, P-08, hard rules #1 #4 #6 #10) | `edge/sync-agent/src/http-transport.ts`, `tests/integration/the-shop-reaches-the-cloud.test.ts` | `SyncTransport` was a port with **no implementation** — "a thin adapter at deployment". Until now the edge committed sales durably before the receipt printed, queued them, reported its unsent count honestly, and had **nowhere to send them**. Every piece was tested apart; the join did not exist. **An event goes to the endpoint that would have handled it anyway**, and there is deliberately **no generic ingest**: a sale posted to `/v1/ingest` would skip the price check against the published pack, the receipt-number clash, the future-dated commit — the whole exception path — and land in the ledger having been examined by nothing, so a sale that syncs late goes through the same door as one that syncs immediately and gets the same answers. An event type with no route is **rejected by name** into the visible dead-letter queue (#6), not dropped and not posted somewhere hopeful. **The outcome mapping is the whole of the risk**, because `retryable` and `rejected` look almost identical in code and could not differ more in the shop: an unsent sale wrongly rejected stops being retried, and the money in the drawer has no cloud record until somebody works the queue. So a **timeout is retryable and is the common case** — a shop on a rural line meets it several times a day, and nothing about a slow link says the sale was bad; an **ambiguous outcome is retryable, never accepted**, because the receiver dedupes on the idempotency key so a duplicate delivery is one effect, whereas guessing \"accepted\" loses the sale silently and that is the one outcome with no way back; 5xx is retryable; a permanent 4xx is rejected so it cannot bury everything queued behind it; **408/425/429 are retryable** (4xx by number, transient by meaning); and **401 is retryable while 403 is rejected**, which is not the same question — an expired token is renewed by the next restart and the sale should still be waiting, while a permission the till does not hold will not appear by itself. The request carries **the event's own key**, minted when the sale was committed at the lane: a key minted per attempt makes every retry a new sale and the server doing everything right banks all of them. The token is never in a message and **the response body is never echoed**, because a body can quote the request and the request carries the header (#4) — asserted on every failure path, with a counter-test proving the token really is sent so the guard is about the messages rather than about its absence. The end-to-end gate drives the **real API surface against real PostgreSQL** with only the socket replaced: the shop trades five sales through an outage, nothing reaches the cloud and nothing is dead-lettered, the pass **stops early** rather than burning every item's attempt budget on a link that is plainly down, the badge tells the cashier the truth, the link returns and all five land — **once**, including when the edge restarted and lost its outbox and re-queued a sale it had already sent, which nothing at the edge can detect and only the cloud's dedupe can save. 15 unit + 9 integration assertions |
| A stream name cannot be two things at once (OB-01, M20, hard rule #2) | `services/api/src/adapters.ts`, `tests/unit/api-stream-names.test.ts` | Stream-per-aggregate composed names with a hyphen, and `delivery-{driver}-{date}` is unambiguous **only while a date is exactly ten characters**. A driver id ending in something date-shaped, or a run date that ever gains a suffix, and two different runs compose to one stream — which means `reconcileRun` settles **cash, against a named person**, on the strength of a coincidence in string lengths. `packages/catalogue` learned the same lesson when joining fields with nothing let two products canonicalise identically. The parts are now joined with a **unit separator the data cannot contain**, and a part that somehow contains one is **refused rather than stripped**, because a stripped separator is a silently different stream — the failure this exists to prevent rather than a smaller version of it. Proved on the adversarial pair the hyphen collapses, on eight awkward driver/date combinations, and across all four kinds of stream, with a tripwire showing ordinary ids still compose. It earned its keep immediately: an integration test holding a hand-typed `reservations-L1` went red, and now composes through the helper. 5 assertions |
| The disk the receipt waits for (P-01, §31, NFR-03, hard rules #1 #2 #6) | `edge/store-edge/src/file-log.ts` | `DurableLog` was a port with a **test double behind it and nothing else** — `commitLocally`, the function that decides whether a receipt may print, was writing to an interface no deployment could satisfy, so the offline-first claim rested on a file that did not exist. **`fs.appendFile` returns when the bytes reach the operating system's cache, not the disk**, and on a back-office PC with no UPS the difference is a power cut between the receipt printing and the platters catching up: the sale is in the customer's hand and nowhere else. Every append is followed by an **`fsync`**, and `fdatasync` is deliberately *not* used — it skips metadata, and for an append the file's **length is metadata**, so the bytes can be on the disk under an inode that still says the file is shorter, which is the same lost sale by a subtler route. The **directory is synced once** at open, because a log that survived perfectly inside a directory that forgot its entry is not a log. Opened `'a'` (O_APPEND) — never `'w'`, which truncates — and the handle is **kept**, because opening per write puts an open, a sync and a close on the one path a customer stands and waits for. `usedBytes` comes from the filesystem rather than a counter, since a counter is right until the process restarts after a crash, which is exactly when the number decides whether the next sale is refused. **A truncated tail is quarantined by name, never parsed, never dropped and never repaired** (#6) — a repaired half-sale is a made-up sale — and truncation is decided by **arithmetic**: each record carries its byte length, so a short read is a calculation rather than a judgement about whether some JSON \"looks complete\". Found while writing it: the first framing escaped newlines so the file could be read with `split()`, which **corrupted every record containing the two characters backslash-`n` — exactly what `JSON.stringify` writes for a line break** — a lost sale produced by the code whose whole job is not losing sales. The length prefix makes escaping unnecessary and the reader now walks by offset. 11 assertions |
| The store edge starts, and trades, with no cloud at all (P-01, hard rule #1, §19, §31, P-08) | `edge/store-edge/src/main.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/start.ts`, `infra/docker/edge.Dockerfile`, `infra/compose/docker-compose.yml` | Every piece of the edge existed and **none of it had ever been started**: no process, no durable log, no transport. A shop could not have run this. The property the whole file exists to demonstrate is **an absence**: with no `CLOUD_API_URL` and no `CLOUD_API_TOKEN` it opens its disk, reports itself ready to sell, and **says plainly that nothing will be synced** — it does not refuse to start, and it does not pretend. The compose service has **no `depends_on` and no required cloud setting**, so the stack can be brought up with the API stopped and the network unplugged and the shop still sells; if the edge needed the cloud to boot, offline-first would be a paragraph rather than a property, and the first power cut with a dead router would prove it in front of customers. `agent` is **null** rather than one pointed at nothing, which would fail a request every fifteen seconds forever. The sync loop is nowhere near the sale path: **passes cannot overlap** (the next is scheduled only after the last returns), the interval **backs off to a five-minute ceiling** so a shop with a dead router is not making a request a second all night and is not waiting hours once it returns, and a pass that throws is reported and the loop kept alive — a dead sync loop is a shop that silently stops syncing. **SIGTERM drains briefly and goes**: nothing is lost by stopping mid-drain, because an unacknowledged item stays pending, which is the whole reason there is an outbox. What a power cut left behind is **named on start-up**, not counted silently. The container **publishes no port** — the edge is not a service anybody calls, it owns a disk and drains a queue — runs non-root on a read-only root filesystem with its data on a named volume, and has **no health check that depends on the cloud**, because a shop whose line is down is trading perfectly well and a check saying otherwise gets the container restarted in the middle of a queue. 10 assertions |
| An idempotency key outlives the process (§31.1, SEC-03, OB-01) | `services/kernel/src/idempotency-store.ts`, `db/migrations/0007_idempotency_keys.sql` | `MemoryIdempotencyStore` carried the comment *"real deployments swap the port for PostgreSQL"* and `main()` was using it. The control it backs is the one that refuses a **different** request sent under an already-used `Idempotency-Key` — what stops a sale of 400 being answered with the stored result of a sale of 250 — and held in memory it fails two ways that **no test would show and every deployment would meet**: a restart or a deploy empties it, so the guard silently stops applying to every key minted before the restart; and two API instances behind a load balancer never share it, so the guard does not apply at all. Neither is a crash. Both are the control quietly not being there, which is the failure mode this repository spends most of its effort making impossible. Now SQL-backed, keyed `(tenant_id, key)`, with **`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` and never an upsert** — the first answer under a key is the answer, because two requests racing under one key would otherwise leave the second's result stored against the first's hash and every later retry would be told about a request that never happened. The table is documented as a **cache and not a ledger**, and the distinction is load-bearing: expiring a row here is not deleting audit evidence (#6), because the event ledger holds what happened and is untouchable while this holds only what we answered — an expired key means a retry arriving days late is executed again, which is safe precisely because every append in the system is keyed and the ledger dedupes. A **tripwire proves the in-memory store forgets across instances**, so the rest is not merely proving that a database persists things. 6 assertions |
| Hard rule #6 protects evidence that must first exist (SEC-03, §27, hard rule #6) | `services/kernel/src/audit-sink.ts`, `db/migrations/0008_audit_log.sql`, `services/api/src/main.ts` | The kernel writes an audit entry for every write and every refusal, through an **optional** port — and the composition root never supplied one, so `writeAudit` returned immediately on every request (`services/api/src/main.ts`). The rule about never deleting audit evidence was guarding a trail that **was never kept**. Not a crash, and nothing any test would have shown; the first anybody would know is the day somebody asks who changed a supplier's bank details. `audit_log` is deliberately **separate from `event_ledger`**: an audit entry is not a business event, it records that somebody asked something and what they were told *including when the answer was no* — and it must hold attempts by callers with **no tenant**, which `event_ledger`'s `uuid` column could not, so `tenant_id` here is text and `'unauthenticated'` is a legitimate value rather than a row that could not be written. It carries the **same append-only triggers** as the ledger (migration 0004's function), because an audit trail somebody can edit is not one, and this is the table where that matters most. A failed audit write is **reported, never rethrown**: it runs *after* the handler, so the effect has already happened — turning it into a 500 would tell the till that a sale which succeeded had failed, manufacturing the exact `wasItSaved: unknown` confusion the three-part error exists to prevent. The request keeps its true answer and the missing entry becomes loud, because a gap nobody is told about is the worst of the three outcomes. 6 assertions |
| No test double may run in a shop (SEC-04, P-01, hard rules #1 #6) | `tests/guardrails/no-test-doubles-in-production.test.ts` | **Five faults of one shape in a single session**, each found by hand and every one invisible to the whole suite: `SyncTransport` a port with no implementation, so the edge queued sales and had nowhere to send them; `DurableLog` a port with a test double behind it, so nothing could write a sale to disk; **no edge process at all**; `MemoryIdempotencyStore` wired in `services/api/src/main.ts`, so the guard against a different request under a used key emptied on every restart and was never shared between instances; and **no `AuditSink` supplied there either**. None is a crash. Every one is a control quietly not being there — the failure mode this repository spends most of its effort making impossible — so finding them one at a time by reading is not good enough. This guard scans the **composition roots** (the four files production actually runs) for anything named `InMemory*`, `Memory*`, `Fake*`, `Stub*`, `Mock*`, `Test*` or `Noop*`, ignoring comments so the ones *discussing* the fault do not trip it, and names every hit at once. It works on names because this codebase names things honestly: a class called `MemoryIdempotencyStore` says exactly what it is. It then asserts the four ports that are optional in a *type* and not optional in a *deployment* are each supplied — audit sink, durable idempotency store, real authenticator, real event store — plus the edge's three, including that **no sync agent is built when no cloud is configured** (the absence is the control, P-01: an agent with an empty base URL would fail a request every fifteen seconds forever and call it syncing). Tripwire on the detector against the exact line that was in the cloud API's composition root that morning. 9 assertions |
| A sale rung up at the lane reaches the cloud (P-01, §31, §31.1, hard rules #1 #6) | `edge/store-edge/src/index.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/sync-cursor.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/main.ts` | **The seam the whole product runs through, and it was not connected.** `createEdgeNode.commit()` wrote a sale to the disk and stopped: durable, correct, and **never queued** — so no sale a lane took ever reached the cloud. Every piece on either side was built and tested, nothing joined them, and **nothing failed**, which is what made it invisible; the end-to-end gate written the same day passed because it enqueued events *by hand*, going round the gap rather than through it. The enqueue now happens **after the durable write, never before**: queueing first would send a sale the lane went on to refuse, so the cloud would hold a sale that never happened and the customer walked away without paying for it — the same reasoning that prints the receipt second. **The outbox is rebuilt from the log on every start**, because the log is the system of record and the queue is a view of it; a queue that died with the process is not something to trust across a restart. That needs an answer to *where the restart begins*, and it is a durable, fsync'd **cursor**: the count of records at the front of the log that are completely finished. It advances only over a **contiguous** prefix — a sale still pending, or one dead-lettered and now a person's problem, holds it where it is, because stepping over one means the next restart never re-queues it and that sale is gone with nothing saying so. An unreadable or negative cursor reads as **zero**, so the failure mode is re-sending (which the cloud dedupes) rather than skipping (which is permanent and silent). Found while writing it: the first `advanceCursor` folded over the start-up snapshot of the log, so a sale rung up **during** the run was not in the list and the cursor never moved — every restart would have re-sent everything ever sold. It reads the outbox instead, which holds exactly the right set in exactly the right order. The gate now starts the edge as the container does, rings a sale through the node a lane talks to, and finds it in the cloud's ledger: queued on commit, delivered on stop, **not** re-sent next start, re-sent after a start where it never got through, and **not queued at all when the lane refused it**. 6 integration + 3 unit assertions |
| The receipt waits for the disk (hard rule #1, P-01, §31, M12) | `apps/pos/src/session.ts`, `apps/pos/src/view-adapter.ts`, `apps/pos/src/browser-entry.ts` | The seam one level above the last: `PosSession.commit()` priced, settled, appended to an **in-memory** ledger and queued to an **in-memory** outbox — all correctly, and none of it on a disk. Its own docstring said *\"commit the sale LOCALLY (hard rule #1)\"* and the function did not do it, **which is the most expensive kind of comment**: a lane that lost power between the sale and the next sync lost the sale, and the cashier had already seen \"Sale complete\". The durable write is now the **first** thing that happens — nothing is appended to the ledger, nothing is queued, and the state does not become `committed` until the sale is on the disk. `commit` is therefore **asynchronous**, and that is the point rather than a cost: the receipt number does not exist until the promise resolves, so there is nothing to print early with, and the guarantee is structural instead of a convention. The original *\"synchronous by construction — a sale never awaits the network\"* conflated two different things; **awaiting a local fsync is not awaiting the network**, and hard rule #1 asks for exactly that wait. A refusal is thrown as `LocalCommitRefusedError` carrying **the cashier's words rather than a code**, and it happens before the receipt exists, so nothing has been promised to anybody — the one place in the product where refusing a sale is the correct answer, correct because of the moment. The port is a **constructor argument and required**, not optional: an optional durable write is one a deployment can forget, and forgetting it is invisible, which is the exact shape of every other fault found today. The browser shell defaults it to a **refusal** rather than to acceptance, because a lane with nowhere to write must not take payment. Proved by recording the order of operations rather than assuming it — disk strictly before ledger and before outbox — and by a refused disk leaving the stock, the queue and the state all untouched. 5 assertions |
| The till screen a cashier can actually use (M12, §27, QG-02, NFR-07, P-07) | `apps/pos/web/app.js`, `apps/pos/web/index.html`, `tests/guardrails/the-till-screen-is-usable.test.ts` | The shell had the right layout and asked its questions through **`window.prompt`, `confirm` and `alert`** — wrong here in three separate ways: a browser prompt is a small text field with a system keyboard over it, unusable with a queue waiting and impossible with gloves; **kiosk browsers block them outright**, so the till would simply do nothing; and they cannot be styled, so the one screen that must be readable across a counter is not. Every question is now an **on-screen panel** with 56px targets — a keypad for quantity and cash, preset buttons for a void reason, because free text at a till is a reason nobody can report on afterwards (M15). **Change due is computed as the cashier types**, in the hint under the entry, because miscounted change is the most common till error there is and nobody should do that arithmetic in their head at speed. **The refusal banner does not fade**: when the disk refuses a sale the cashier is told *do not take payment*, which is the one sentence in this product that must not be missed, so it is full width, `role=\"alert\"`, not colour alone, and stays until acknowledged — a toast that disappears after four seconds is a message that was missed by the person serving a customer. The words on a refusal are **the model's `laneMessage`, unchanged**, because rewording them in the view would put a second, untested version of that sentence in the product. **The scanner is a keyboard**, so digits are collected globally and flushed on Enter with **no input to focus** — losing focus sends a scan into whatever was last tapped, which at a till is a quantity field, and a barcode typed into a quantity is a sale of nine hundred million units. Tamil covers **every** word, not a subset, because a half-translated screen reads as unfinished exactly where somebody is relying on it. Guarded statically: no `prompt`/`confirm`/`alert` anywhere, no timer on the banner, a declared ≥48px touch target, words as well as a dot on the sync badge, every English key present in Tamil, the scan path global, the commit awaited, and **no pricing or tender arithmetic on this side of the boundary**. 12 assertions |
| Card, UPI, and a held basket (M12-FR-03, M12, §27, hard rule #3) | `apps/pos/src/session.ts`, `apps/pos/src/view-adapter.ts`, `apps/pos/web/app.js` | A hypermarket takes mostly card and UPI, so a cash-only till is not a till. **Three outcomes, and the third is where money is lost:** approved completes the sale, declined does not, and **no answer is neither** — the terminal has not come back, the customer is waiting, and the temptation is to read silence as success. A tender the model marks `uncertain` does not count as paid, `commit` refuses, and **the goods stay on the counter**; the screen says so in words rather than showing an error code. No \"force complete\" exists anywhere, proved by scanning the module's exports, because the moment one does it is used at seven on a Saturday. **A real ordering bug, caught by the test rather than the eye:** the durable write ran *before* the payment was checked, so a card payment the terminal never answered was written to the lane's log and only then rejected — and the edge rebuilds its send queue from that log on every restart, so it would have carried sales that were never paid for to the cloud. The order is now **decide, then record, then account for it**: validation is not an effect, nothing has happened yet, and nothing needs to survive a power cut. It is the same `settle` that `commitSale` uses, so the two cannot reach different answers about one basket. **Hold and recall** park a basket whole — a screen that silently emptied is how one customer's items get rung up twice — and the screen *says* a basket is held rather than showing an empty one. The card-data guardrail caught the first draft of this file's own comment for naming a card field outright; rewording the prose rather than relaxing the rule is the right way round, and it is why the rule is still worth having. 8 assertions |
| The drawer is counted blind (M15, M13, §27, QG-02) | `apps/pos/src/till-session.ts`, `apps/pos/web/app.js`, `tests/unit/pos-till-session.test.ts` | Everything the lane does that is not ringing up a basket: money in and out of the drawer, refunds, and closing the shift. **The cashier never sees the expected figure before they count**, and that is structural rather than a habit — `expectedMinor` is **not a method on the till session**, so there is nothing to call, nothing to render early, and nothing for a later change to expose by accident; the expected total exists only on the *result*, once it can no longer influence what somebody wrote down. Shown \"expected: ₹6,000\" people write ₹6,000 — not from dishonesty, but because a number on a screen is an answer and counting is work, and a cash-up anchored to the expectation finds nothing, which is the one thing a cash-up exists to do. Same control as the stock count, and worth being structural in both. The drawer is counted **by denomination** with plus and minus buttons rather than one typed total, because a typed total is a number somebody worked out in their head at the end of a shift. A material variance produces an **instruction, not a figure** — *do not put the money away, call the manager now* — since \"variance ₹200\" is a fact and only the instruction gets acted on at the end of a long day; an immaterial one passes without a reason, because demanding one for every rupee trains people to type anything and then the reasons on the material ones mean nothing either. **A card refund stays `pending`**: cash and store credit settle at the lane, but a card reversal is something the provider has to perform, and showing a completed refund for money that has not moved is how a customer is told they have been paid back and finds out days later that they have not. Receipt-based returns are **said rather than hidden behind a dead button** — they need the original sale and the lane cannot look one up yet, so the screen sends the customer to the service desk. 13 unit + 5 guardrail assertions |
| The manager's screen, and a day that will not close on an assumption (M02, M07, M09-FR-04, M14-FR-04, M15, §27, §28, QG-02, P-03, P-08) | `apps/web-erp/src/manager-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/src/browser-entry.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/`, `tests/unit/erp-manager-session.test.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-manager-boot.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-manager-screen-is-usable.test.ts` | Without this screen goods cannot be received and the day cannot be closed. The engines existed and were tested; what had never been driven was the **join**, and the join is where this project's faults have all lived. **The decision the whole file exists to hold:** `closeDay` takes `unresolvedExceptions: number` and `unsentSyncItems: number`, and nothing stops a screen passing `0` — a screen that could not reach the exception register would pass zero for exactly the same reason a clean store does, and the day would lock, **locked**, on an assumption. So this session does not read counts. It reads **registers**, and a register may answer *I do not know*, which is a third answer distinct from *none*; not knowing is a blocker, and it is the strongest one there is. An unwired screen therefore refuses to close the day and says why — that is the correct behaviour, not a degraded mode to tidy up later. **The second decision: "cannot close" is useless at eleven at night.** Every blocker is computed and enumerated *before* the engine is called, so the manager gets *3 sales unsent, 2 exceptions open — here they are* in one pass rather than one refusal per attempt; the list is never truncated by the model, only by the view, which says how many more. If the engine refuses when the screen predicted nothing would, that **disagreement becomes a visible blocker** rather than a stack trace — the day stays open either way. **The blind count again, and harder:** there is no method on this session that returns an expected quantity, so there is nothing to render early; and because a variance is *valued* to decide whether it needs a second approver (§28), an unknown cost is refused rather than defaulted to zero — priced at ₹0 a shrinkage of any size sits below every approval threshold there is and posts with nobody's approval at all. A delivery with **no purchase order is said, not filed quietly**: stock still rises because the goods are in the building, but nobody can match an invoice to an order that does not exist and the buyer can still fix that today. Approve and reject carry **separate reason catalogues** so "against policy" can never be recorded as an approval, and the **code** is what reaches the audit trail because "ok fine" cannot be reported on a year later. **A real bug the assembly test caught:** `branchId ?? 'store-1'` quietly demoted a company-wide manager to one branch, where their own scope then blocked them from deciding anything — `null` means company-wide and only `undefined` means unconfigured. Two tripwires now bind the model's vocabularies to the view's words: every `BlockerKind` and every `DecideRefusal` must have English and Tamil, so adding one and forgetting the translation fails the build instead of showing a manager a blank reason at the moment they most need one. 45 unit + 30 guardrail assertions |
| The owner decides from a phone, on data that left the shop hours ago (M29, M29-FR-02, D13, M02, §28, §31, §31.1, QG-02, P-03, P-08) | `apps/owner-app/src/owner-session.ts`, `apps/owner-app/src/browser-entry.ts`, `apps/owner-app/web/`, `packages/approvals/src/reasons.ts`, `tests/unit/owner-session.test.ts`, `tests/unit/owner-app-boot.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-owner-app-is-honest.test.ts` | `apps/owner-app/src/brief.ts` already built the day's numbers and the three things needing attention; what did not exist was anything that made them safe to **act** on. **The decision the file holds:** the owner is remote and every figure here is historical by construction — last-synced data, with the shop trading since it left. Reading the day on eleven-hour-old numbers is fine; approving a ₹8 lakh purchase order on them is not, and the difference between those two uses of one screen is the whole design. So a decision now **carries the ground it was made on**: `freshnessAtDecision` and `dataAgeSeconds` go into the decision itself, because *approved, on data eleven hours old, and he was told so* is a different fact from *approved* and only one of them can be defended later. On stale data the approve panel carries the age in words and the owner must act through it — **not a block**, because an owner who cannot approve while travelling telephones instructions instead and a telephoned approval has no audit trail at all. **One thing is blocked:** deciding when nothing has ever synced, since `missing` is not old data but *no* data and an acknowledgement of nothing is not informed consent. **The §31.1 case, built:** a decision made with no signal is queued, and while it waits the request can change or be withdrawn — so the queue is an **input** to the session (it outlives the app on a phone, and a queue lost silently is the worst way for an approval to disappear), and `reconcileQueued` compares each answer against the question as it now stands. A ₹40,000 approval that quietly applied to a ₹90,000 request is the exact failure maker-checker exists to prevent, so those come back to the owner: **never silently sent, never silently dropped.** The fingerprint that detects it is joined on a unit separator rather than concatenated, because a control that can collide is not one. **Three faults in the shipped shell, each invisible in review:** it answered a tapped alert with `window.alert('t-1\nt-2')` — a system dialog holding raw ids, a dead end wearing the clothes of an action; it rendered a fabricated ₹413 of takings **with no warning that they were sample figures**; and its language button set `document.documentElement.lang` and changed **not one word**, a toggle that says translated and is not. All three fixed and guarded. Every figure now drills to **every** sale behind it — never a sample, because a drill-through that disagrees with the figure it drills is only ever discovered by the person being asked to act. The decision vocabulary moved to `packages/approvals` so the manager's screen and the owner's phone write **one** vocabulary into one audit trail. **A repository-wide fault found on the way:** every guardrail had been scanning `*.bundle.js` build artifacts, so the suite passed or failed depending on whether somebody had run a build — the one thing a guardrail may never do. Excluded narrowly, with a test proving the hand-written shell beside it is still scanned. 50 unit + 32 guardrail assertions |
| The handheld and the driver's phone — and work that had nowhere to go (M19, M18, D09, §28, §31, §31.1, QG-02, P-01, P-08, hard rules #3 #6) | `apps/picker-app/src/pick-session.ts`, `apps/picker-app/src/browser-entry.ts`, `apps/picker-app/web/`, `apps/delivery-app/src/route-session.ts`, `apps/delivery-app/src/browser-entry.ts`, `apps/delivery-app/web/`, `packages/sync/src/device-outbox.ts`, `tests/unit/handheld-work-queues.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-handheld-screens-are-usable.test.ts` | **The fault first, because it is the point.** Both session docstrings said the scans, the proof and the COD *"queue for sync afterwards"*. **Nothing queued.** Every resolved pick line and every completed stop lived in a JavaScript object on a device and nowhere else — the ninth and tenth instance in this project of a control that was described, believed and absent, and neither was caught by a single existing test. On the picker's handheld that loses an afternoon. On the driver's phone it loses **money that has already changed hands**: ₹6,000 collected across four stops, a dead battery, and no record anywhere that any of it was ever collected — the settlement has nothing to reconcile against, which is unfair to an honest driver and invisible for a dishonest one. The outbox is now a **required constructor argument** on both, because an optional port is one a deployment forgets and forgetting it is invisible; queueing happens in the single `replace()` each session funnels every state change through, so a path added later cannot skip it. `DeviceOutbox` writes through to the device after **every** mutation — enqueue, acknowledge, failure and dead-letter — because a decorator forwarding only `enqueue` would persist the work and lose the fact it had been sent, and the next boot would send all of it again; state and attempts are restored exactly, so a dead letter stays dead (hard rule #6). Restoration is a **constructor argument and nothing like it exists as a method** — an outbox whose contents can be rewritten after the fact is not evidence of anything. **Two model gaps the screens exposed.** `substitute` took `customerConfirmed: boolean`, and a boolean on a handheld is a tick box a picker with eleven lines left taps in half a second — true in the type system, unverifiable in the aisle. It now requires the customer's approval **reference**, which travels with the swap so a disputed substitution can be looked up rather than argued about; the screen scans it and has no free-text box at all. And the driver handed cash over with nothing counting it, so `handOver` now reconciles a **counted** figure against the recorded collections — **the blind count for the third time**, with no method returning the expected cash before a count, exactly as the till drawer and the stock count do it. **The screens:** no text input anywhere in the picker shell (the only way "every pick is a scan" survives a busy afternoon is if a crushed label cannot be typed around), global scan capture, a three-step indicator that actually advances — a guardrail caught the first version defining words for steps 2 and 3 and rendering only step 1, which is worse than no indicator because a picker told "step 1" while standing at step 3 stops reading it. Proof is asked for before money because that is the order it happens at a door; **card is not on the payment list at all**, so the engine's hard-rule-#3 refusal is one a driver can never walk into with a customer waiting; a failed delivery must say what happens to the goods next. What leaves either device is deliberately thin (§31): the order reference, the state, the money and the proof **kind** — never a customer name, never a phone number, and never the doorstep photograph, which would be a privacy problem being uploaded rather than a delivery being proved. 47 unit + 43 guardrail assertions |
| The warehouse handheld — receiving and put-away that decide on the served facts (M09, OA-9, §28, §31, §31.1, P-01, hard rules #1 #2 #10) | `apps/warehouse-app/src/warehouse-session.ts`, `apps/warehouse-app/src/browser-entry.ts`, `apps/warehouse-app/src/index.ts`, `tests/integration/warehouse-screen-fed.test.ts` | The owner's OA-9 decision is a dual-interface warehouse: a scanner-first offline PWA for the floor and a Web ERP supervisory surface, **both on the same authoritative services with no duplicated logic**. This is the first honest half of the PWA — the offline execution engine and the assignment feed it boots on — built **data-path first**, with the visual shell (bilingual strings, service worker, socket wiring) as the next work package. **The decision the file exists to hold: it orchestrates, it never re-decides.** Receiving is `packages/receiving`'s `receiveScan` unchanged — an unknown barcode goes to a resolution queue rather than becoming a closest match, a case is converted exactly or refused, a price edited at the door is refused (the three-way match depends on the agreed price), and an **over-delivery or a direct-store-delivery needs a SEPARATE approver** (§28: the approver can never be the receiver, checked on the payload the box served). Put-away and bin capacity are `packages/warehouse`'s `applyMovement`/`suggestPutAway` unchanged — an **unknown bin is queued for resolution, never invented** ("somewhere near aisle 4" is how stock becomes unfindable), a **full bin and an over-draw are refused** rather than going negative, and the item scanned must match what is waiting to be put away. Recall and expiry are `packages/fefo` — a recalled batch or an expired one is folded into the stock **state** so the one authoritative gate keeps it out of any pickable bin, and the worker is told **which** rule stopped them (recall vs expiry) rather than a generic refusal; the goods still go somewhere safe (a holding bin), because they are in the building either way (hard rule #10 — a refusal is a visible exception, not a silent drop). **Every accepted action queues for idempotent sync and a refusal queues nothing** (append-only, #2): a receipt queues `GoodsReceived`, a put-away queues `WarehouseMovementApplied` **keyed on the command id the cloud's movement ledger also keys on**, so a re-sent scan reconciles to one movement (hard rule #1 — commit locally first, sync idempotently). The outbox is a **required** constructor argument, exactly as on the picker handheld, because an optional queue is one a deployment forgets and forgetting it is invisible. Every scan returns a **feedback signal** — accept/warn/reject with a sound and a vibration length — so a rejected scan is felt as well as seen by someone in ear defenders in a cold store (OA-9 scan confirmation). A duplicate scan is a **warn**, not a reject, because it is a harmless no-op and the worker should not be told they did something wrong. 10 integration assertions |
