| The customer's app, and leaving made exactly as easy as arriving (M20, M16, D07, D08, A04, PRV, §31, QG-02, NFR-07, hard rules #3 #4, DPDP Act 2023 s.6(6)) | `apps/customer-app/src/privacy-centre.ts`, `apps/customer-app/src/browser-entry.ts`, `apps/customer-app/web/`, `tests/unit/customer-privacy-centre.test.ts`, `tests/unit/customer-app-boot.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-customer-app-is-honest.test.ts` | The last surface with no screen, and the only **public** one — used by a stranger on a slow phone who will never read a manual, and the one place where the commercial incentive points the wrong way. **The control this screen exists to hold is section 6(6) of the DPDP Act 2023:** the ease of withdrawing consent must be comparable to the ease of giving it. That is the commonest dark pattern in consumer software and it is never built deliberately — it arrives one reasonable decision at a time, because it costs nothing to add a confirmation on the way out and something to add one on the way in. So the symmetry is **structural**: one `setConsent` taking the direction as an argument, no `withdrawConsent` for a reason field and a retention offer to accumulate on, one switch per purpose on screen, and a guardrail that **counts the click handlers in the consent renderer** and names the four shapes the pattern actually takes. A withdrawal is recorded as a withdrawal rather than by deleting the grant, so the shop can still show what it was permitted to do and when (M16-FR-02); consent reads OFF until granted, because a pre-ticked box is not consent. **Second: a request that was RAISED is not one that was DONE.** The phone can raise an access, correction, export or erasure request with nobody contacted (QG-02) — and it comes back `raised` and **unverified**, which is correct rather than a gap, since a phone that verified itself would be no check at all and an unverified access request hands over somebody else's shopping history. The screen shows the answer-by date and never says the data is gone. **Third: an erasure is almost never total, and the limit is on the button before it is pressed.** Tax invoices, GST records and audit evidence survive one by law; `planErasure` already said so *afterwards*, and somebody who taps "delete everything" believing everything goes has been misled even though every later step was accurate. **Fourth, and the spine of the shopping half: prepared is not placed** — the deliberate inverse of the till. At a lane we commit locally and sync after, because the money is in the drawer and the customer has gone; here nothing has happened at all, so a basket prepared with no signal says *not sent yet, nothing has been charged*, and the guardrail forbids the view from holding any sentence claiming an order was placed. `reachedTheShop` is the transport's answer and the view is checked for never hard-coding it true. The basket caches on the device (product ids and quantities, nothing worth stealing); prices never do — the review carries the catalogue pack version and paying against an older one is refused rather than quietly repriced (P-02). WCAG 2.2 AA is enforced statically: a skip link, a focus outline that cannot be deleted, a label for every input, and **no font, image or script loaded from anywhere else** — which is both a slow-connection decision and a third party not being told who shops here. 37 unit + 29 guardrail assertions |
| The store box feeds all six screens — the join that had never been made (§31, §31.1, P-01, P-02, P-08, M14-FR-04, M15, M29-FR-01, ADR-0004) | `edge/store-edge/src/store-pack.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/read-model.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-server.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/main.ts`, `tests/unit/edge-feeds-the-screens.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-box-never-invents-an-answer.test.ts` | Six screens were built to read a global at boot and **nothing had ever set one**, so all six sat honestly reporting that they had been told nothing. The box now tells them, and the two registers the manager's day close gates on are **produced here rather than fetched**: `unsentItems` is this box's own outbox (nothing else in the system can know what has not reached the cloud — by definition, the cloud cannot), and `openExceptions` is the day evaluated locally against the store's own thresholds, because waiting for the cloud to notice a refund spike would blank the register exactly when the line is down, which is when a shop is least supervised. **The day close now closes**, proved end to end over a real socket against the real shells on disk — the loop that had been correctly refusing for three sessions. **The one-character rule, held on the box's side:** every pack section is a `Register` that is either known (contents may legitimately be empty) or not known (with the reason); `?? []` is banned by a guardrail in the payload builders, because it converts *"the cloud has never mentioned approvals"* into *"no approvals are waiting"* and the day close turns on which. **Margin is only served where it can genuinely be worked out:** cost price is a fact about the catalogue, not the sale, so a sale containing a product with no cost is **excluded from the margin figures and counted**, with the products named on the owner's own screen — costing it at zero reports a 100% margin, a lie that reads as very good news and would be believed. Takings stay complete either way, so both numbers are true and the gap between them is stated. **A real defect this fixed in the lane:** the durable record carried the basket and the payable total and dropped net, tax and the tender kind — all three already computed to take the money, then thrown away, leaving the day's figures underivable from the disk. **A real defect it found in the customer app:** `searchCatalogue` returns the hits array and the view read `result.hits`, so `?? []` made **every search report "nothing matched"**, for every term, including exact barcodes — nothing threw and no test failed; the shop merely appeared to stock nothing. **And one more of the lane-socket kind:** the pack carries `valueMinor` where the approval engine takes `Money`, so the manager's inbox threw on `value.currency` — the same shape mismatch as `saleId` against `id`, found the same way, by driving the path. The socket is loopback-only (it carries the day's takings, the exception register and the price list), GET-only, path-traversal guarded with decode-before-check, and the payload is escaped so a product name typed into a spreadsheet cannot close the script element. 53 unit + 19 integration + 20 guardrail assertions |
| Dispatch — routes planned on the box, and every order accounted for (M19-FR-03, M19-FR-04, D08, D09, §31, P-01, P-08) | `packages/fulfilment/src/routing.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/store-pack.ts`, `tests/unit/dispatch-routing.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-box-never-invents-an-answer.test.ts` | Nothing planned a route, and the consequence was worse than a driver holding a handwritten list: `reconcileRun` was handed an empty assignment list, so **every delivery a driver actually made came back as one nobody had dispatched** — goods out of the building against orders no run could account for. `assignedOrderIds` closes that, proved against the real reconciliation. **The invariant is not the sequencing, it is that every order goes somewhere:** onto a route or onto the unplanned list with a named reason, never both and never neither, because a dropped stop is a customer who ordered, paid, waited and was told nothing, and finds out by nothing arriving. `accountedFor` is asserted against the input and driven as a property across five shapes of day; a guardrail additionally checks **no `continue` in the planner discards an order without recording it**. **It says what it is:** there is no map, no road network and no traffic — the arithmetic is great-circle geometry, so a river or a one-way system between two points makes the ordering wrong. Every plan therefore carries `distancesAre: 'straight_line'` in the *result* rather than in a comment, and it is a **draft a dispatcher confirms**, never an optimum it cannot compute. **Windows beat geography:** the customer booked a slot, so the promised window is the outer ordering and nearest-neighbour runs inside one — optimising across windows produces a shorter route and a broken promise, and only one of those is visible on a screen. Capacity and shift hours are constraints rather than suggestions (a driver given forty stops in a two-hour window fails twenty-five and the shop finds out at nine in the evening), a stop whose estimate lands outside its window is **flagged and still delivered** — it is a conversation, not a reason to quietly not deliver somebody's shopping — and a van off the road triggers a **full re-plan** rather than appending its stops to another run in whatever order they happened to be in. Deterministic on every input, because a dispatcher who re-runs it and gets a different answer stops believing either. **Dispatch runs on the store box**, not in the cloud: a shop whose routes could only be planned when the internet was up would stop delivering on the afternoon the router dies (P-01). A hand-written route still **wins** — software that cannot be overridden gets worked around, which means a driver with a piece of paper and a screen that disagrees with it — and the screen is told which of the two it is showing. An order the planner could not place lands on the manager's exception register and therefore **holds the day close**, which is correct: a day should not lock with somebody's shopping still in the building and nobody having told them. Reuses the one existing `metresBetween` rather than adding a third haversine. 24 unit + 6 integration + 7 guardrail assertions |
| Every screen opens without a network — and says which page you are looking at (§31, P-01, P-08, M12, M19, M20, M29, ADR-0004) | `apps/pos/web/sw.js`, `apps/web-erp/web/sw.js`, `apps/owner-app/web/sw.js`, `apps/picker-app/web/sw.js`, `apps/delivery-app/web/sw.js`, `apps/customer-app/web/sw.js`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-server.ts`, `tests/guardrails/every-screen-opens-without-a-network.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | Six service workers were written, are correct, and **five of them were never registered by anything** — so nothing was ever cached and every screen fell back to its sample stand-in the moment the box was unreachable, which at the goods-in door and on a delivery round is most of the time. It failed visibly rather than dangerously (the sample strip says *this is not your shop*), which is why it survived three sessions. **Switching them on would have been a regression on its own**, and that is the finding worth keeping: these screens are served with the shop's data injected into the page, so a cache-first page is a cache-first *payload* — this morning's exception register handed back as this minute's, silently, by the one mechanism nobody was looking at. So the page is **network-first** and only falls back when the network genuinely fails; the copy that goes into the cache is stamped with the time it was taken and every screen renders a strip saying so, in English and Tamil, in the reader's own local time; and the live copy handed to the browser is untouched, so online there is no strip and nothing changes. **A missing script is never answered with a page** — HTML where JavaScript was asked for is a syntax error, and the screen then boots into its stand-in for a reason nobody can see. The bundle is cached at last (without it "offline" meant *offline into the sample data*) and added tolerantly, because `addAll` is all-or-nothing and one 404 stopped the whole install. **Two more faults found by writing the guardrail rather than by reading:** the picker's and the driver's workers shared the cache name `sre-handheld-shell-v1`, and `activate` deletes every cache that is not its own — served from one box, they took turns wiping each other; and a bare `/pos` was served **200 and broken**, because without the trailing slash a browser resolves `./pos.bundle.js` against `/`, so the page opened with no bundle, no view and no worker registered, and nothing anywhere said why. The box now redirects `/<screen>` to `/<screen>/`, query string intact, before it reads anything off the disk. The till's own promise is untouched: this cache never sees a non-GET, never sees the lane's loopback socket, and the strip tells a cashier **billing still works** rather than stopping somebody who could have carried on serving people (hard rule #1). 19 guardrail + 8 integration assertions |
| The product and price screen — and the price nobody had ever made (M03-FR-01/02/03/04, M05-FR-01/02/03/04, D01, D06, §28, §29.1, QG-02, P-02, P-07, P-08, hard rule #2) | `packages/price-list/src/price-change.ts`, `packages/product/src/completeness.ts`, `apps/web-erp/src/catalogue-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/catalogue.html`, `apps/web-erp/web/catalogue.js`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-catalogue-session.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-pricing-screen-cannot-break-the-law.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | `resolvePrice` read a price, `priceHistory` reported on them, `checkPrice` said whether one was allowed and `buildCatalogueSnapshot` shipped them to the lane — and **nothing had ever produced one**. Every `PriceEntry` in this repository was a test fixture, so the snapshot builder had never had a real price to build from and no lane had ever been given a price this system decided. Same shape as the supplier invoice nobody captured: every rule built and tested, the join missing, nothing failing to say so. **The refusal that nothing may authorise:** a price above MRP is rejected outright and there is no approval branch, because MRP is a legal ceiling in India rather than a shop policy — and the ceiling read is the one **in force today**, not the newest on the record, since a future MRP increase must not raise today's ceiling before the pack it is printed on reaches the shelf. A guardrail additionally asserts that no `overrideMrp`/`forcePrice`/`ignoreMrp` function exists anywhere on the surface: a helpful-looking escape hatch here is a standing offer to break the law with an audit trail proving it was deliberate. **The refusal that had no name until now:** `checkPrice` needs a landed cost, and a product with none has no knowable margin — costing it at zero makes every price look like 100% margin, so the floor check passes confidently and wrongly exactly where a buyer relies on it. An unknown cost is therefore its own verdict, escalated to a separate approver rather than assumed away, and the box serves **no entry at all** for a product with no cost rather than a zero. **Append-only, enforced:** a change is a new entry with a higher version and its own effective date; the entry it supersedes is returned untouched, a withdrawal appends a `rolled_back` entry rather than deleting, the proposal hands back a `draft` that `resolvePrice` will never select, and a guardrail bans in-place assignment to `.status`/`.price` (its first draft matched `===` and would have fired on correct code — fixed and tripwired). **Back-dating is refused** and no approval can authorise that either: a price active from yesterday changes what `resolvePrice` says yesterday's sales should have charged, so the receipts and the reports stop agreeing and nothing says which is right. **The completeness score (D01) is counts first and a percentage second** — a bare "68%" gets put on a wall and decided upon and nobody can say what the other 32% is — and the checks come from the **tenant's own** category attributes and regulated declarations, never from an opinion in this repository about what a Tamil Nadu hypermarket ought to record. A product in a department the screen was never told about reports **not knowable with the reason**, not 0%: a zero reads as *somebody has filled in nothing* and sends a person to fix a record that may already be finished. The two limits — the printed MRP and the lowest price that keeps the margin — are rendered **before** the price field rather than in the refusal afterwards, because a screen that only says "rejected" teaches people to guess. Separation of duties is held in three places: the box strips the setter from the approver list, the view filters it again, and the rule refuses their approval whatever the screen offered. 46 unit + 10 integration + 37 guardrail assertions |
| Shelf addresses, and the picker's walk that nothing had ever sequenced (M04-FR-02, D02, D05, M19, §31, QG-02, P-01, P-08) | `packages/merchandising/src/shelf.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `apps/web-erp/src/catalogue-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/catalogue.js`, `apps/picker-app/src/pick-session.ts`, `apps/picker-app/web/app.js`, `tests/unit/merchandising-shelf.test.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-catalogue-session.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-pricing-screen-cannot-break-the-law.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | Built on the owner's decision of 6 August 2026 — shelf locations before go-live, planograms and display contracts deferred. `ShelfMap.routeFor` had existed and been unit-tested since the module was written and **nothing anywhere called it**, so every wave was walked in whatever order the cloud sent it, which on an online grocery order is the order the customer typed: dairy, rice, back to dairy. The roadmap's audit calls picking time the largest controllable cost in this business and it is decided here — third instance this session of a control described, tested and never connected. **A second fault inside the rule itself:** `ShelfLocation.zone` carried the comment *a picker collects chilled last (D05)* from the day it was written, and the sort only ever compared aisle/rack/bay/shelf/position — the field was decoration, and a chiller that happens to sit in aisle 1 was picked first with the milk then carried round the shop. The walk now sorts **zone before position**, and a zone the store did not list is collected last rather than first, because being sent for the unfamiliar thing at the end costs a minute and being sent for it first can cost a trolley of chilled goods. **The order is the store's, never ours:** there is no default cold-chain order and a guardrail asserts none is ever introduced — which zones a shop has, how far apart they are and how long chilled goods may stand out are questions about a licensed premises, and the wrong guess is silent (the route looks sensible and the milk is warm). Absent a zone order the walk is ordered by position alone and **says so**, in the result rather than in a comment, the same discipline as the driver's route saying whether a dispatcher wrote it by hand. **An unmapped line goes last, is marked on the handheld and is named** — hiding it sends the picker back across the shop and dropping it loses the line — and the maintenance screen lists every product with no shelf address, because the person who can fix it is the person reading it. Two homes for one product is refused on the rule and on the screen (the picker's route and the refill task would disagree about where an item lives, and then both are wrong); a pack carrying that contradiction has the **one bad row dropped rather than the whole map**, since refusing everything would report every product in the shop as unmapped and read as the shelf data having been lost. Sequencing runs on the **box**, so a shop whose pick lists could only be ordered online does not go back to walking dairy-then-rice on the afternoon the router dies (P-01). Addresses stay numbers rather than labels: as text "A10" sorts before "A9" and the picker walks the aisle twice without ever knowing why. **Not built and named rather than dropped:** planograms, compliance, replenishment tasks and supplier-funded display space (M04-FR-03/04) — owner-deferred, and the replenishment half additionally has no producer of on-shelf counts anywhere in the system yet. **The zone order is now answered for tenant #1** — `ambient → secure → chilled → frozen` (OB-07, 6 Aug 2026), held as `SETTINGS.PICK_ZONE_ORDER` with an **empty** product default and proved end to end against a shop laid out to punish it: every zone in the wrong physical aisle, so a route ignoring the zones returns the exact reverse. Every rule was written and tested the day the module was created — the range drop, the assortment integrity check, the planogram comparison, the refill tasks, the space ranking, the display contracts — and **not one was called by anything outside its own unit test.** Fourth instance this session. **The fault that gated the whole build, and the reason it was flagged as a prerequisite before the owner chose:** `planogramCompliance` needs how many of an item are on the shelf right now, nothing in this system had ever produced that figure, and its `state?.onShelfMinor ?? 0` therefore turned every **uncounted** facing into an **empty** one — which is the loudest finding the function has, *the sale is being lost with the stock in the building*. On day one, before anybody had counted anything, that fired for **every product in the shop** and sent staff to full shelves. An alarm that goes off on everything is one people learn to ignore, and then it is worse than no alarm at all. So `packages/merchandising/src/shelf-count.ts` was built first: a **blind** count — the function accepts no expected quantity, so none can leak back to the screen, and a test asserts nothing on the surface returns one — recorded **append-only**, because *we counted it at nine and again at two* is the record that explains a variance and overwriting the nine o'clock reading destroys the only evidence of what changed in between. **A shelf quantity is an observation, not a fact:** `ShelfState.observedAt` is required, and a count older than the tenant's own window raises **no task at all** rather than sending somebody on a three-day-old reading — enough wasted walks and the whole list stops being believed. An unreadable timestamp counts as stale, which is the safe direction. **The compliance figure is over the facings actually counted**, `notObserved` says how many were left out and `wholePlanObserved` says whether the figure means what it says; a plan nobody has counted returns **0%, not 100%**, because an empty plan is not a compliant shop but an unchecked one. The screen renders the coverage **above** the percentage and adds, in words, that a partial check says nothing about the rest of the shop. **Two nothings are distinguished:** no shelf map and no planogram lead to different actions, so `check()` returns which rather than "0 issues", which would read as neither and as a clean shop. **On-hand is served from the pack's own availability** and a range drop reads it: serving nothing would have been the dangerous default, turning *route to clearance* into *delete* and making the stock on the shelf invisible — not counted, not replenished, eventually written off. A ratio that cannot be computed says *not measured* rather than zero, because "0 sales per sq ft" and "we never measured this area" lead to opposite decisions. The range check compares against **this box's own sales log**, the one figure on the screen that does not come from the cloud. 22 unit (shelf) + 15 unit (count) + 22 unit (session) + 13 integration + 32 guardrail assertions |
| Reporting and analytics — and the report that would have opened blank (M29-FR-01/02, D13, API-10, M30-FR-02, §32, QG-07, P-03, P-08) | `packages/reporting/src/catalogue.ts`, `apps/web-erp/src/reporting-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/reporting.html`, `apps/web-erp/web/reporting.js`, `edge/store-edge/src/read-model.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-reporting-session.test.ts`, `tests/unit/edge-feeds-the-screens.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/a-report-nobody-can-run-is-named.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | The pieces existed and were tested — `figure()` cannot be constructed without the moment it is true as of, `salesSummary` aggregates KPIs, `exportDomain` writes an audited, permission-checked, PII-redacted file — and there was nowhere a person could run a report, and nowhere that stated **which reports this shop cannot run at all**. D13 names 26; nine have code behind them. A screen listing only the nine looks finished: somebody asks for shrinkage, finds nothing, and concludes either that the shop has none or that the software forgot, and neither reading is correctable by anybody looking at the screen. So **every report is listed and an unavailable one is refused by name**, in the shop's own words — *nothing yet records what is thrown away* — which is the build backlog stated in business language. **The fault found inside the work, and the fifth instance of the session's recurring shape:** the first cut judged availability on the shop's records alone, so a tenant who started recording stock counts would have opened *Shrinkage*, satisfied every stated need, and been handed a page with no figures and no rows — under the report's real name, with a real as-at stamp on nothing, because the producer switch ended `default: return { figures: [], rows: [] }`. An empty shrinkage report reads as **no shrinkage**. A report is now runnable only when **both** halves hold — the shop records the facts AND this build computes them — `PRODUCED` names the second half, a test asserts it against the `case` labels in both directions, and the fallback **throws** rather than serving an empty dashboard. **The two gaps are told apart because they have different owners:** *nothing yet records that* is the owner's to fix and *this version cannot work it out yet* is ours, they carry different refusal codes, different words in both languages and a different colour, and a report we cannot build is kept off the owner's backlog entirely — he would do the work the screen asked for, open the report and find it blank. An empty backlog no longer reads as *every report works*. **Driven over the real socket, which found a second silent fault:** the payload had its own copy of the line-costing rule and divided by a thousand unconditionally — right for weighed goods, wrong for everything countable — so a ₹100 cost became 10 paise and the margin report read **99.92%**, the exact "a 100% margin is a lie that reads as very good news" failure arrived at from the other side. Nothing failed; the number was simply wrong. `lineCostMinor` and `basketUnits` are now exported from the read model and there is one implementation. **A bill with no readable lines** keeps its takings, which are real, and carries **no** basket size — a zero would be averaged into units-per-basket — and the count of those is reported beside the figure, as the margin already does for uncosted sales. **Nothing is written out under a name nobody holds:** an export's audit record is the only evidence afterwards of who took the shop's data, so the box serves the user the pack names and an absent one refuses the export outright rather than denying under a default id. **The comparisons of M29-FR-02 followed** once the day boundary existed to make them meaningful: *today against the last day we traded*, which **refuses when the box holds no earlier day** — a comparison against a day that is not there reports the shop as having doubled overnight against a nought nobody put in — and takes the previous day as the most recent *strictly earlier* one, so a future-dated sale from a lane with a wrong clock cannot become the thing today is measured against. The change is stated in **money rather than as a percentage**, because a percentage off a small day is a large number that means nothing on a screen people quote from. And *what is selling, by department*, in **units rather than money**: the log records a quantity per line but no money per line, so department revenue could only be rebuilt from list prices and a bill carrying a promotion would then produce a department total that does not add up to the day's takings — a figure that nearly reconciles is worse than one that is honestly a count. An item the catalogue places in no department is counted apart, never folded into one, because hiding it inside Grocery is how a catalogue problem stays invisible. 48 unit (session) + 13 unit (payload) + 17 integration + 41 guardrail assertions |
| The day boundary — the box had none, and the mistake compounded daily (M29-FR-01/02, M14-FR-04, M15, §31, QG-07, P-08, hard rule #10) | `edge/store-edge/src/read-model.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `apps/web-erp/src/reporting-session.ts`, `tests/unit/edge-feeds-the-screens.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/a-report-nobody-can-run-is-named.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | Found by driving the reporting screen against a box that had been trading for four days rather than one. **`sales.log` is append-only and never rotated**, so `readSales` returns every sale the box has ever committed — and every builder that meant *today* was handed all of it. The owner's phone reported ₹2,245 as the day's takings on a day the shop took ₹145; "Sales by day" reported the same; and the manager's exception register — **which the day close gates on** — counted a refund from last Tuesday against today's limit, so on a box a fortnight old a shop in which nothing at all went wrong could no longer close its day and nobody could clear it. Nothing crashed. No test went red. The number was simply wrong, and it got wronger every day the box stayed up. `salesOn(sales, tradingDay)` is now the one boundary and `managerPayload`, `ownerPayload` and `reportingPayload` all cross it; a guardrail asserts each one does and that none still costs or judges `input.sales` whole, with a tripwire on both shapes. **The trading day is the SHOP's, not the calendar's** — it runs to the tenant's own cutoff, so a sale rung at half past midnight belongs to the day before, and the screen is told the day rather than deriving it from a clock. **A sale naming no trading day is in nobody's figures and says so**: including it puts another day's money in today's takings and dropping it silently loses real takings from a total somebody reconciles against the till roll, so it is excluded and **counted**, on the manager's register and on the owner's brief, exactly as an unreadable record already was. **The whole log is deliberately kept where the whole log is the right answer** — the range check asks whether this shop has *ever* sold something it does not range, and an item that sold last Tuesday is exactly as much evidence as one that sold this morning. 6 unit + 3 integration + 6 guardrail assertions |
| Customer service and returns — and the refund that could be given every day (M13-FR-01/02/03/04, M21-FR-03/04, API-05, API-06, §28, §30.2, QG-07, P-01, P-08) | `packages/returns/src/return-register.ts`, `packages/returns/src/returns.ts`, `apps/web-erp/src/service-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/service.html`, `apps/web-erp/web/service.js`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `tests/unit/returns-register.test.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-service-session.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-same-goods-cannot-come-back-twice.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | The till has said *"refunds against a receipt need the original sale, and this lane cannot look one up yet — send the customer to the service desk"* for as long as it has had a refund button, in both languages, and **there was no service desk**. `packages/service-desk` (SLA assessment, compensation maker-checker, AI-draft approval, satisfaction, service report) and `packages/reversal` were both called by nothing outside their own tests: the sixth and seventh instances of this codebase's recurring shape. **And the one that costs money directly:** `commitReturn` has enforced *a line is returned at most once* since the day it was written, against `alreadyReturnedMinor` — **supplied by the caller, and outside one unit test nothing in this system ever supplied it.** Every return in the running product was therefore judged against *nothing already returned*, so the same receipt could be refunded today, again tomorrow, and again the day after, each refund passing a rule written specifically to stop it. `packages/returns/src/return-register.ts` is that producer. It aggregates **by product rather than by line index**, because a bill can carry one product on two lines and matching on an index would let "line 2" be returned twice while "line 1" is untouched; it counts a shared return id **once**, because the box's log and the cloud's history overlap by design and double-counting an overlap turns a legitimate second return into a customer refused at the desk; and it is projected from **both** sources, because the cloud alone leaves the guard blind exactly when the line is down. The `ReturnAccepted` event carried `lineCount` and not the lines, so nothing downstream — the cloud included — could compute the figure at all; it now carries them, additively. **Two guards, not one:** the line rule stops the same *goods* coming back twice and `alreadyRefundedMinor` stops the same *money* going out twice, and a discounted bill can pass the first while failing the second — two ₹400 refunds against a ₹500 bill are two different units. **A card refund is never called refunded**: the provider has to move it and offline nobody has even asked, so it is recorded pending and the customer is told it takes a few working days, rather than being handed a receipt that makes the shop responsible for a promise it has not kept. The reversal itself stays in the service that can reach the provider — a browser has no business holding a provider credential. Damaged goods do not re-enter sellable stock; nothing commits under a name nobody holds; a material or no-receipt refund needs a **different** person; and **a line with nothing left is shown greyed with the reason rather than hidden**, because the agent has to be able to answer the only question the customer will ask. 16 unit (register) + 37 unit (session) + 10 integration + 32 guardrail assertions |
| Expiry and recall — and the recall block that never reached a till (M10-FR-01/02/03/04, D05, §9.3 FSSAI, API-04, API-06, QG-02, P-01, P-08, hard rule #6) | `apps/web-erp/src/expiry-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/expiry.html`, `apps/web-erp/web/expiry.js`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/store-pack.ts`, `packages/fefo/src/fefo.ts`, `packages/traceability/src/traceability.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-expiry-session.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/a-recalled-tin-cannot-be-sold.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | `allocateFefo`, `expiryActions`, `assessColdChain`, `releaseFromQualityHold` and `traceBatch` were all written, all tested, and **not one was called from anywhere outside its own unit test** — the eighth instance of this codebase's recurring shape. **And then the one that was worse than unwired.** `CatalogueCache.assertSellable` refuses a recall-blocked scan *before it checks anything else*, and its message says **"even offline"** — the loudest safety claim in this system. `PackProduct` had **no field for the flag to arrive in**, and `posPayload` served a shape that was not a `CatalogueSnapshot` at all: no `barcodes` array, no `status`, no `taxBps`. Proved by driving it rather than reading it — `new CatalogueCache(payload)` **threw on boot**, so on a real box a cashier got a blank screen with nothing anywhere saying why, and once that was fixed there was still no recall flag to honour. **A recalled tin scanned and sold like any other.** The integration test that should have caught it checked the payload's *contents* and never that the lane could consume them, which is exactly how it survived; it now builds the real `CatalogueCache` and scans through it. The flag is read from the lane summary **and** from `productMaster.recallBlocked`, and **either one saying blocked means blocked** — a safety flag must fail one way only. A product with no tax rate or no status is **excluded from the lane and named**, because a guessed tax rate is a wrong number on every bill for that item where an unknown barcode is at least a question somebody asks — but a **recalled** product is shipped *with* its block rather than excluded, so the till refuses it by name and not by absence. **The screen itself:** the expiry action list M10-FR-01 asks for first, earliest expiry leading, expired to dispose and near-expiry to mark down against the tenant's own window; FEFO allocation that never draws expired stock; and recalls built on the backwards trace. **A recall is not finished when it is started** — blocking the till is the easy half. The headline figure is **what is still in customers' homes**, not what is on the shelf, and the shop is told how many buyers it can contact *and how many it cannot*, because four of nineteen is what decides whether a public notice goes up. Closing needs **evidence of what was actually done with the stock**, and closing with stock unaccounted for needs a second note saying why — in a real recall some of it has been eaten and is never coming back, and that is a decision somebody signs rather than a default. The reason goes **into** the closure record, which is all that survives. A closed recall is never edited; a correction is a new record. **An empty expiry list is distinguished from a shop that records no batch dates at all** — only one of those is good news. 22 unit + 7 integration + 26 guardrail assertions |
| Finance — the control totals that made a month closeable at all (M23-FR-01/02/03/04, D10, QG-07, API-09, §28, P-08, hard rule #2, hard rule #6) | `packages/period-close/src/control-totals.ts`, `apps/web-erp/src/finance-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/finance.html`, `apps/web-erp/web/finance.js`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/store-pack.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-finance-session.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/a-month-cannot-close-on-nothing.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | The roadmap's acceptance for this module is one sentence: **a CA can sign the control totals.** `validateControlTotals` compares both sides exactly, `closePeriod` returns **every** blocker at once rather than one per attempt, and `buildEvidencePack` writes the pack for the person signing it — all three written and tested the day the module was created, and **none of them ever given a control total, because nothing in this system built one.** The gap analysis has carried the consequence in plain words for weeks: *no month can close yet.* `packages/period-close/src/control-totals.ts` is that producer, and this screen is where a person uses it. **The decision the whole thing turns on:** only a posting the accounts have **accepted** counts as received. A queued posting is money Tally has never seen; a dead-lettered one is money Tally refused. Counting either would make both sides of every total agree — the same number computed twice — and the month would close, reconciled and signed, with the accounts missing the lot. That is the *vacuously satisfied* failure this codebase keeps finding, in the one place where a chartered accountant puts their name to the result; the queue is therefore reported **beside** the totals and never inside them, and a guardrail asserts `buildControlTotals` mentions neither pending nor dead-lettered value. **The two sides are independent by construction** — the ledger side is the shop's own record, never derived from the postings, because two sides from one source always agree and the check is then theatre — and each total states **how each side was derived**, so a CA can re-derive it without asking anybody. **No ledger side means no totals at all, not an empty list**: an empty list reconciles vacuously, and a tripwire in the guardrail proves `closePeriod` really does close on one, so the guard is load-bearing rather than defensive decoration. A posting matching none of the shop's headings is raised as **"postings nobody can classify"** rather than quietly left out, and the headings are the tenant's own chart of accounts, never a constant. **A closed month is append-only:** reopening needs a reason and a different person's approval, and that control is **on the screen** — a capability the session has and the screen does not is the fault this codebase keeps finding, so it is shown the moment the month is closed. Nothing here can discard a refused posting; they are listed in full with the reason the accounts gave. 26 unit + 8 integration + 25 guardrail assertions |
| Admin and security — and the copy of a security control that enforced less (M01, M02-FR-01/02/04, M33, M34-FR-01/02, D12, §27, §28, SEC-11, QG-02, P-04, hard rule #4, hard rule #6) | `apps/web-erp/src/admin-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/admin.html`, `apps/web-erp/web/admin.js`, `services/platform/src/index.ts`, `packages/platform-admin/src/support-access.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/store-pack.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-admin-session.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/support-access-is-never-standing.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | The design bar for this surface is one line: **time-bound, audited support access — never standing god-mode** (P-04). Two things stood between that sentence and the running system. **First, the control had two implementations, and the weaker was the one wired to the API.** `services/platform` carried its own `grantSupportAccess` whose request had **no `scopes` field at all**, so access granted over the wire could not state least privilege, could not be refused for holding a scope support may never hold, and had no rule stopping an approval **lengthening** the window that was asked for. The package's version enforces all three. A second, simpler copy of a security control is the one that drifts, and it drifts in the direction of letting more through; the service now maps HTTP to the one implementation and nothing else, and a guardrail with a tripwire asserts it never defines its own again. **Second, the expiry was never checked.** `supportSessionActive` decides liveness from the clock — *"expiry is a fact about time, not an event someone triggers"* — and nothing outside its own unit test ever asked it, so a session was granted with an `expiresAt` in a response body and no code anywhere read it again: standing access wearing a time limit's clothes. The screen computes it on every render and never stores it, because a stored flag has to be turned off by something and that something is exactly what did not exist. Alongside them, `accessReview`, `evaluateDevice`, `fleetSummary`, `planRetention`, `holdApplies`, `liftHold` and `validateVersionPolicy`: seven more written-and-tested rules called by nothing — the eleventh instance of this codebase's recurring shape. **What the shop has not decided is never reported as decided:** no version policy means **nothing is being enforced**, not a compliant fleet, so no device is judged against a minimum nobody set; and no retention policy means **nothing has been decided**, not nothing due for deletion. A legal hold outranks a retention date and nothing on this surface deletes anything (hard rule #6), support sessions are never pruned, and nothing is granted under a name nobody holds. 21 unit + 8 integration + 26 guardrail assertions |
| AI control — and the kill switch that stopped nothing (M32-FR-01, M36, A01–A10, AI-NFR-01…12, API-13, §28, QG-02, P-05, P-08, hard rule #5) | `packages/ai/src/gateway.ts`, `apps/web-erp/src/ai-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/ai.html`, `apps/web-erp/web/ai.js`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/store-pack.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-ai-session.test.ts`, `tests/unit/ai-gateway.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-kill-switch-actually-stops-it.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | The design bar for this surface is one line: **AI recommends or drafts; deterministic rules and authorised humans commit — and a kill switch that always works** (P-05). **Every AI governance rule in this product was called by nothing outside its own tests** — `grantTools`, `reviewProposals`, `commitProposal`, `admitCall`, `summariseSpend`, `killSwitchState`, `activateKillSwitch`, `liftKillSwitch`, `runEvalSuite`, `liveProviderGate` and `scanForInjection` — the twelfth instance of this codebase's recurring shape, and the one where the whole module *is* governance, so unwired governance was the module doing nothing. **And one of them was worse than unwired.** `killSwitchState` computed which agents were stopped and `admitCall` refused a killed, over-budget or switched-off agent; `callModel`, the one place every model call in this product passes through, **consulted neither**. Pulling the kill switch stopped nothing: the switch existed, the state was computed, the screen would have drawn it in red, and the gateway carried on calling the provider. The gateway now **requires** a `CallAdmission` and **its absence refuses**, checked before the transport is touched, because an emergency control that depends on every caller remembering to consult it is not an emergency control — all sixteen existing gateway tests failed on that change, which is what proves the guard is load-bearing. **The screen is built around the switch:** it is the first tab, it needs a name and a reason but never an approval (a kill switch that waits for approval is pulled twenty minutes too late), lifting it is a separate act by a **different** person, and beside every switch is the sentence saying what the shop does without that assistant — *"what breaks if I do this?"* is the only question anybody will ask at eight in the evening. **What no assistant may ever do is shown as a list**, because a rule nobody can see is a rule nobody trusts, and the screen grants and reviews through the same functions the runtime uses so it cannot show a tool the runtime would refuse. **A thirteenth instance, found by driving a box that had been running a month:** spend had **two sources** — a carried per-agent map with no period attached and the metered call log — and on that box the assistants tab read 95,000 spent while the cost tab beside it read nought, for the same agent, on the same screen; worse, that figure is what `admitCall` uses to decide whether an agent may spend again, so a carried total refuses an agent all month for July's spending and a missing one hands it the whole ceiling afresh. There is now **one source**: the metered calls, filtered to tenant and period. **Absent is never nought** — no ceiling and no metered spend are told apart and each says which, because they need different fixes; an assistant missing from the evaluations has **never been checked**, not scored nought; and no platform ceiling (D3) means **no summary at all**. 45 unit (session) + 20 unit (gateway) + 11 integration + 29 guardrail assertions |
| Migration — the cutover gate that had only ever been told (MG-01…MG-12, M30, §34, §34.1, WF-19, QG-07, OD-05, OD-06, API-09, §28, P-01, P-08, hard rule #1, hard rule #6, hard rule #7, hard rule #10) | `packages/migration/src/cutover-checklist.ts`, `apps/web-erp/src/migration-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/migration.html`, `apps/web-erp/web/migration.js`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/store-pack.ts`, `packages/contracts/src/event.ts`, `tests/unit/migration-cutover-checklist.test.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-migration-session.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-cutover-gate-is-never-ticked.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | The design bar is one line: **no cutover without signed control totals, every exception kept, and a rollback that has been performed rather than designed.** `decideCutover` is the gate on the single most irreversible act in this project — the night SRE Hyper Market stops running on the old system — and it refuses GO until all eight checks pass, names every failure at once, and types `shopKeepsTrading` as the literal `true`. **It takes those eight as booleans supplied by the caller, and nothing in this codebase ever derived one.** Both integration tests and its own unit test — *including the test that stands as the Stage 11 gate evidence* — hand it a literal with `qg07Passed: true`, `parallelRunSufficient: true`, `edgeUnsyncedItems: 0` typed in by hand. The gate on the whole migration had never once been asked about the state of the migration; it had only ever been told. Every one of the eight already had a real producer beside it — `assessReconciliation`, `parallelRunPosition`, `outstandingExceptions`, the box's own outbox — and **`packages/migration/src/cutover-checklist.ts` is the join that was missing**, alongside fifteen more MG functions (`inventorySources`, `approveMapping`, `resolveException`, `signControlTotal`, `assessRetirement` and the rest) called by nothing outside their own tests: the largest instance of this codebase's recurring shape, and the one that matters most because of OB-06 — *the owner extracts his own data himself*, and a pipeline that can only be driven from a test file is a migration nobody can run. **NOT KNOWN FAILS:** every input is optional and every absence is a failure, never a comfortable default — no `?? 0` on the unsynced count, because "nobody told me" and "there are none" are opposite facts and only one is good news — and an **unanswerable** check is reported and drawn differently from one that came back **bad**, because a failed check is work to finish and an unanswerable one is a producer that was never connected. **A defect this screen introduced, found by driving it against a box mid-migration rather than reading it:** `sign` computed a signature, the view showed it green, and the next render re-read the payload and drew the total unsigned again — a signature that lived only in a browser tab. It is now **committed locally and queued** (hard rule #1, idempotency key `tenant:type:subject` so a replay collapses to one effect), the screen draws the working copy, and what has not reached the store computer is **counted on the page** (P-08). The person who ran the load can never sign its totals (§28) — and a box never told who ran it signs nothing at all; finance and tax totals are the CA's alone; a blocking exception is cleared by a decision including a decision to carry it, never by being unexamined, and is resolved and kept, never deleted (hard rule #6); the rollback is one clearly-labelled action needing nobody's approval, at any time, in any state, and retiring the legacy system never deletes the legacy data (MG-12). 21 unit (checklist) + 36 unit (session) + 11 integration + 32 guardrail assertions |
| Deployment (§19, §20, SEC-04/06, ADR-0002, hard rule #4) | `services/kernel/src/config.ts`, `services/kernel/src/http-server.ts`, `services/api/`, `infra/docker/api.Dockerfile`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml` | **A service with a missing or placeholder secret does not start.** It does not warn, substitute a default, or carry on reduced — a system that boots with a default signing key is a system running in production with one, and the log line saying so scrolled past three months ago. Everything is checked **at boot rather than on first use**, and **every problem is named at once**, because one per restart produces five deploys and a guessing game. The placeholder check reads the values that actually ship in `.env.example` — that file is what gets copied and forgotten — and a test reads the example file from disk so a newly added placeholder cannot slip past the refusal list. **Liveness and readiness answer different questions**: a database it cannot reach makes the API *not ready* and still *live*, because restarting will not reconnect a database and a crash loop buries the outage that caused it. **The store edge's required config contains no cloud setting at all** — if it needed the cloud to boot, offline-first would be a claim rather than a property (P-01). On SIGTERM the server **drains**: a request cut off mid-flight is a sale that reached the process and not the database while the till believes it was delivered. The container runs as a named non-root user, read-only root filesystem, no-new-privileges. CI **builds the image and proves the refusals**, expecting exit 78 (EX_CONFIG) and checking the message names every missing setting, then brings the whole stack to `/readyz`. Tested in `tests/integration/it-actually-starts.test.ts` — which boots on a real socket, since everything else in the suite is proved without one |
| The buyer's screen — and the eighty lines nobody has to retype (M06, M07-FR-04, M30-FR-01/03/04, D03, §28, A-03, QG-02, P-07, P-08) | `apps/web-erp/src/buying-session.ts`, `apps/web-erp/web/buying.html`, `apps/web-erp/web/buying.js`, `packages/purchasing/src/three-way-match.ts`, `edge/store-edge/src/screen-data.ts`, `tests/unit/erp-buying-session.test.ts`, `tests/guardrails/the-buying-screen-is-honest.test.ts`, `tests/integration/the-screens-are-fed.test.ts` | Every piece existed and was tested — `packages/purchasing` issues an order, `packages/import` validates a file and commits it atomically, `packages/receiving` books goods in, the three-way match pays the lowest of three — and **nothing captured a supplier invoice**, so `matchLines` returned an empty set for every one and the match refused every time, correctly and uselessly. Audit finding A-03 is somebody retyping an eighty-line invoice by hand every week, and this is the replacement. **The control that makes bulk capture safe rather than merely fast is the total printed on the paper.** The buyer types it off the bottom of the supplier's own document, and it is the only figure in the flow that does not come from the file — which is exactly why it can catch a line the file is *missing*, where every remaining line is perfect and nothing else in the system would notice. Proved end to end: a clean file short by one line previews with zero problems and is still refused. **Each line's own arithmetic is checked too** — quantity × unit price must equal the printed line total, reporting the source **line number** so somebody can go and look at the paper; the generic import engine validates types and references and has no idea what those three columns mean to each other. **Nothing writes before the preview and somebody else's approval**: the save button does not exist on the page until the model says the file may be saved (absent, not disabled — a disabled button is a thing to keep clicking), the commit is atomic because seventy-seven of eighty lines written is an invoice matching no piece of paper anywhere and nobody can say which three are missing, and a self-approval is refused (§28). **A real fault caught by its own test:** `match()` built its line set from the union of ordered ∪ received ∪ invoiced, so an invoice nobody had captured still produced rows with invoiced quantity zero and came back *not blocked* — the engine's own "an empty line set is not agreement" guard, defeated from the outside by handing it lines it should never have had. It now passes zero lines, and three documents cannot agree when we are holding two of them. **The buyer is stripped from their own approver list on the box**, not on the screen that would offer them, because separation of duties enforced only by the list somebody was shown is not enforced at all. **And what the box did NOT say is said**: every missing section here fails toward a refusal, which is the safe direction and still not honest on its own — "this was never ordered" looks identical whether the supplier invented the line or the box was never sent the order, and only one of those is an argument to have with a supplier, so each gap is named in a sentence on the page in both languages. The three-way match **moved from `services/purchase` into `packages/purchasing`** so the screen and the service run the same rule: a browser cannot import the HTTP kernel, and the alternative — a second copy of "what may we pay" — is one of them being wrong, found by a supplier paid one figure and reconciled against another. 25 unit + 12 integration + 27 guardrail assertions |
## Assembly state — what "Built" above does and does not mean

The family table certifies **domain logic and its tests**. It does not certify a system anybody can
switch on, and reading it alone would leave that impression. Measured, not estimated:

| Layer | Source | State |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| `packages/` — domain logic | ~44,800 lines, 247 files | **Complete.** Every module M01–M36, 2,673 tests |
| `apps/pos` | ~990 lines + PWA shell, service worker, manifest | **Runnable shell.** Lane guards, session, view adapter, offline cache |
| `apps/web-erp` | ~5,700 lines + **eleven** PWA shells (767-line manager view, 580-line buyer view, 700-line product/price view, 560-line shelves/space view, 394-line reporting view, 430-line service-desk view, 380-line expiry/recall view, 400-line finance view, 380-line admin/security view, 420-line AI-control view, 480-line migration view), service worker, manifest | **Runnable shell.** Approvals inbox, receiving, blind count, day close by exception; the buyer's screen (supplier-invoice capture against the printed control total, the three-way match, raising an order); the product and price screen (completeness scoring, publish, recall block, an effective-dated price change against the MRP ceiling and the margin floor, price history, promotion simulation); the merchandising and space screen (blind shelf counts, refill tasks that never fire on an uncounted facing, the range review, what each part of the floor earns per square foot); the reporting screen (every report D13 names, the ones this shop cannot run listed as prominently as the ones it can and saying which of the two gaps it is, and an audited export); **and the service desk** — receipt lookup from the box's own log, the at-most-once return guard fed at last, refunds that never claim a card has been repaid, and complaints with their SLA clock and compensation under maker-checker. **and expiry and recall** — the action list, FEFO, and a recall that reports what is still in customers' homes and will not close without evidence. **and finance** — both sides of every control total, the Tally queue beside them and never inside them, and a month that closes only when every figure agrees. **and admin and security** — outside access that is scoped, time-bound and judged live from the clock; who can get in; the fleet; and what the retention rules would do. **and AI control** — a kill switch that now actually stops a model call, what each assistant may do and what no assistant may ever do, everything it has drafted waiting for a named person, and what it has cost against the owner's own ceiling. **and migration** — the eight-check cutover gate DERIVED from the real state rather than ticked by hand, the figures signed by somebody who did not load them, the old data's problems kept and decided, and a rollback that needs nobody's approval. One bundle, eleven pages, because these are eleven jobs for eleven people (P-07) |
| `apps/owner-app` | ~1,020 lines + PWA shell, service worker, manifest | **Runnable shell.** Brief, drill-through, approvals with a freshness-aware decision, queued-decision re-check |
| `apps/picker-app` | ~800 lines + PWA shell, service worker, manifest | **Runnable shell.** Scan-driven picking, evidenced substitution, quality, packing with cold-chain |
| `apps/delivery-app` | ~1,000 lines + PWA shell, service worker, manifest | **Runnable shell.** Stops, proof, COD, failure routing, blind cash handover |
| `apps/customer-app` | ~1,100 lines + PWA shell, service worker, manifest | **Runnable shell.** Search, repeat order, basket review, slots, payment, privacy centre |
| `edge/sync-agent` | ~208 lines | **Built.** Ordered, idempotent drain with retry, backoff and a visible dead letter |
| `edge/store-edge` | ~1,940 lines | **Built.** Durable local commit before the receipt prints; capacity shed order that never touches a sale; **and it now feeds all six screens** from its own log plus the last pack, saying which of the two it is answering from |
| `services/kernel` | ~700 lines, 33 tests | **Built.** The shared foundation all thirteen APIs sit on: versioned paths, default-deny permissions, mandatory idempotency, the three-part error, tenant isolation and the no-card-data guard |
| `services/catalogue` — API-02 | ~330 lines, 30 tests | **Built.** Signed offline pack: publish refusals, lane accept/reject, last-known-good |
| `services/pricing` — API-02 | ~120 lines | **Built and persisting (M05-FR-02/FR-04, recovery Phase 3).** Governed price change: MRP ceiling rejected outright, below-cost/below-floor blocked unless a SEPARATE person who genuinely holds `price.change.approve` (checked against the tenant's grants) signs it with a reason; the setter cannot self-approve (§28). An allowed change is an append-only `PriceChangeRecorded` event; the pack-publish path re-checks §28 at the shelf edge. **Now also hosts promotion governance** (`services/pricing/src/promotions.ts`): simulate the margin impact, and launch a margin-losing offer only with a named approver (≠ proposer) and a written reason (§28) — a loss-leader on purpose, never by accident. **And the effective-dated, scoped price list** (`services/pricing/src/price-list.ts`, M05-FR-01): publish an append-only, versioned, effective-dated entry at a scope (customer/channel/zone/store), resolve the one price that applies at a moment by precedence (returning the entry to lock a sale to), and read the chronological history — above MRP refused at any scope (legal ceiling), back-dating refused. **And the promotion catalogue & best-price basket evaluation** (`services/pricing/src/promotion-catalogue.ts`, M05-FR-03): a promotion is DEFINED as a draft, deliberately ACTIVATED and can be STOPPED (ended, not paused), and `POST /v1/promotions/evaluate` runs the deterministic `bestPrice` over the active in-window set — only published+eligible apply, exclusive-group best-only + stacking, member/coupon gates, never below zero. `tests/integration/price-change.test.ts`, `tests/integration/promotion-governance.test.ts`, `tests/integration/price-list-effective-dating.test.ts`, `tests/integration/promotion-catalogue.test.ts` |
| `services/pos` — API-05 | ~230 lines, 25 tests | **Built.** Sale intake: always banks, never corrects, everything wrong becomes a visible exception. **Now also hosts returns** (`services/pos/src/returns.ts`, `POST /v1/sales/:saleId/returns`): a refund is guarded where the whole history lives — at-most-once against everything already returned on the bill (feeding `packages/returns`' register at last), a refund never above what was paid, and a material refund needs a second person (§28); a card refund is reported pending, never assumed settled. **And till cash + shift close** (`services/pos/src/cash.ts`, `services/pos/src/shift.ts`): float/loan/pickup/safe-drop/return with one custodian per till and no overdraw (`POST /v1/tills/:tillId/cash-movements`), and the blind shift close — expected vs count, over/short valued, a material variance needs a reason (`POST /v1/shifts/:shiftId/close`, `GET /v1/shifts/over-short`). Proven in `tests/integration/returns-guard-the-refund.test.ts`, `tests/integration/till-cash.test.ts` and `tests/integration/shift-close.test.ts` |
| `services/inventory` — API-04 | ~180 lines | **Built.** Append-only movements, projected balances, negative stock reported not blocked. **Now also hosts the packaging back-office** (`services/inventory/src/packaging.ts`, M28-FR-03): `POST /v1/packaging/items/:id` + `…/movements/:mid` register a packaging item and its received/issued/returned/written-off movements, and `GET …/position?branchId=` runs `projectPackaging` — a returnable projected IN CIRCULATION with a loss rate, a negative on-hand shown negative not clamped (the evidence a goods-in was missed), per branch; the bag CHARGE stays offline in the price pack (hard rule #1). Proven in `tests/integration/packaging.test.ts`. **And the waste & sustainability report** (`services/inventory/src/waste.ts`, M28-FR-04): `POST /v1/waste/records/:id` + `POST /v1/waste/coverage` log valued waste and the expected reporting units, and `GET /v1/waste/report` + `…/compare` carry COVERAGE on the face of the number — `not_comparable` below 80% with the silent departments named, a comparison that refuses to call a fall an improvement when coverage moved. A store-staff record vs an accountant read split. Proven in `tests/integration/waste-reporting.test.ts` |
| `services/identity` — API-01 | ~120 lines | **Built.** Scope resolution, maker-checker grants, no credential stored anywhere |
| `services/platform` — API-11 | ~170 lines | **Built.** Health that distinguishes alive from working; time-bound support access. **Now also hosts facilities schedules** (`services/platform/src/facilities.ts`): cleaning/pest/fire/electrical/statutory tasks where a tick without required evidence is refused, a self-verified safety check is refused (§28), and a compliance-linked miss escalates by itself while cleaning never does. Proven in `tests/integration/facilities-schedules.test.ts`. **And the integration gateway** (`services/platform/src/integration.ts`, M32-FR-04): `POST /v1/integration/matrix/:id` + `…/adapters/:id` register the certified matrix and adapters — a payment adapter retaining card data or a literal credential refused with no override, an uncertified/non-RBI provider refused — `GET …/devices/check` names a certified alternative, and `GET /v1/integration/health` catches an adapter silent for days while `posUnaffected` stays true. Proven in `tests/integration/integration-gateway.test.ts`. **And inbound signed webhooks** (`services/platform/src/webhooks.ts`, M32-FR-01): `POST /v1/integration/webhooks/:provider/deliveries/:id` runs `verifyWebhook` — a forged or wrong-tenant delivery is a 401 security event, a stale one 422, a duplicate a 200 provider-retry ack, the HMAC key resolved from a vault ref. Proven in `tests/integration/webhooks.test.ts`. **And connector mapping validation** (`services/platform/src/connectors.ts`, M32-FR-02): `POST /v1/integration/connectors/:id/mappings/:version/validate` runs `applyMapping` — an unmapped field, a lookup miss or a missing required field is named rather than silently run. Proven in `tests/integration/connector-mapping.test.ts` |
| `services/purchase` — API-03 | ~200 lines | **Built and persisting.** A supplier invoice's lines are now **captured on the cloud surface** — `POST /v1/purchase/invoices/:invoiceId/capture` writes `PurchaseInvoiceCaptured`, and the match folds exactly those lines — so `/match` answers against real data instead of an empty stream that refused every invoice; the capture is idempotent per invoice, so a re-send collapses rather than doubling what the supplier is owed. Three-way match pays the lowest of three and holds back the difference, and an invoice nobody captured is refused as *not checked* — a different answer from *checked and clean*. Bank change verified out of band, dated, and recorded as an event rather than an overwrite. What is on order answers *not known* — purchase orders are not on this surface yet (M06). The match rule itself lives in `packages/purchasing/src/three-way-match.ts` and is re-exported here, so the buyer's browser screen runs the **same** implementation as the service rather than a copy of it. (The buyer's ERP screen captures into the **offline store-edge pack**; this endpoint is the **cloud** counterpart — the edge→cloud sync of captures is a separate wire.) **Now also hosts the supplier portal** (`services/purchase/src/supplier-portal.ts`): nothing a supplier submits takes effect on its own — catalogue/RFQ/claim land requiresReview, an ASN/invoice needs the grant + compliance, another supplier's order is refused, a retry is a duplicate; the partner's grants come from stored config, never the payload. **Compliance is checked AT THE ACTION** (M24-FR-03): the partner's real documents (with expiry + verification) sit in its config and `checkPartnerCompliance` runs at the submission, so an expired/missing/unverified required document blocks the very next ASN/invoice while an expiring one only warns, and `GET …/compliance?asOf=` shows a buyer why. **Also hosts the supplier statement** (M24-FR-04): `POST …/statement/opening` + `…/statement/lines/:ref` record the opening and signed lines, and `GET …/statement` runs `buildStatement` — a closing balance from named buckets cross-checked (`reconciles`), a dispute shown separately, and a login without `view_statement` answered with a permission answer not a zero balance. Proven end to end in `tests/integration/purchase-capture-match.test.ts`, `tests/integration/supplier-portal.test.ts`, `tests/integration/supplier-portal-compliance.test.ts` and `tests/integration/supplier-portal-statement.test.ts` |
| `services/finance` — API-09 | ~200 lines | **Built and persisting.** A closed period is closed — and open is the *absence of a close*, folded from the stream, so no period can be open and closed at once. Journals, closes and who posted into a month are all real. **Control totals are not**: no genuine second source exists inside this API, so the close refuses rather than closing unchecked. **Now also hosts settlement** (`services/finance/src/settlement.ts`): a provider batch is refused unless it reconciles to its own declared figures and is not a duplicate (`POST /v1/settlement/batches`), and the day's card/UPI tenders are reviewed against what the provider paid, telling apart not-due-yet from late and valuing short/over/unknown (`GET /v1/settlement/review`); the review reports the window it read, never silently calling an out-of-window tender settled. An exception then becomes an **owned, evidenced, outcome-closed investigation** (`POST /v1/settlement/investigations`, `…/:id/evidence`, `…/:id/resolve`, `GET …/investigations`) — a mutable aggregate folded from its events, with a §28 write-off control and ageing buckets. **And B2B credit control** (`services/finance/src/b2b-credit.ts`): a per-customer credit limit and an AR balance projected from invoices/payments, with an over-limit order or expired contract blocked pending a separate approver (§28). **And B2B collections** (`services/finance/src/b2b-collections.ts`, M22-FR-04): structured receivables aged from the DUE DATE, a payment allocated oldest-due-first with the overpayment held unapplied (settled projected from the allocations), a disputed invoice never chaseable, and dunning that recommends stop-supply for a person to commit — proven in `tests/integration/b2b-collections.test.ts`. **And B2B commission** (`services/finance/src/b2b-commission.ts`, M22-FR-03): a salesperson's commission EARNED not stated — `POST /v1/b2b/commissions/:sp/accruals/:id` computes the payout on the server with the exact-money engine (half-up, cap after the round), `GET …/:sp` reports the total projected from the accruals never a stored balance, a re-send collapses on the accrual id — proven in `tests/integration/b2b-commission.test.ts`. **And the B2B document chain, part 1** (`services/finance/src/b2b-documents.ts`, M22-FR-02): a quotation issued on its OWN gap-free series drawn ONCE and only on success (a rejected quotation leaves no gap — the engine validates without a number, and a number is allocated only if it would issue), then converted into a sales order at the QUOTED price through the REAL credit gate (`checkCredit`: no account → blocked, over-limit → blocked, sufficient → cleared) and refusing a second conversion of the same quote — proven in `tests/integration/b2b-documents-chain.test.ts`; and part 2 — a proforma (never tax-claimable), a challan carrying what LEFT with over-delivery refused, the tax invoice built from the CHALLANS (partial delivery bills partially, an over-invoice refused), and the chain check naming delivered-but-not-invoiced — proven in `tests/integration/b2b-documents-chain-2.test.ts`. **And concession** (`services/finance/src/concession.ts`): a partner's counter charged rent/revenue-share/higher-of-both exactly, settled with the tills' takings as a **liability never revenue**, deposit stated-not-netted, till-vs-counter difference a valued exception. **And scrap** (`services/finance/src/scrap.ts`): scrap/recycling proceeds made to EXIST — recorded not refused, off-books cash and rate drift flagged, posted-to-finance clears the flag. Proven in `tests/integration/settlement-cash-office.test.ts`, `tests/integration/settlement-investigations.test.ts`, `tests/integration/b2b-credit.test.ts`, `tests/integration/concession.test.ts` and `tests/integration/scrap.test.ts` |
| `services/reporting` — API-10 | ~110 lines | **Built.** Read-only; no figure without its as-at; not-available never rendered as zero |
| `services/customer` — API-06 | ~140 lines | **Built and persisting.** Consent decided at use, with given/withdrawn/given-again all kept — the middle one is what a regulator asks about. **Loyalty points are now wired**: `POST /v1/customers/:id/points` earns/burns/reverses money-like append-only movements, the balance is projected (never stored) and a burn can never take it below zero; a customer with no movement still answers *not known* rather than zero, so the distinction survives the fold. **Gift cards / store credit are wired too** (`services/customer/src/stored-value.ts`): a card is issued with an opening value, redeemed never-overdrawn / expiry-refused / offline-capped / idempotent, and its balance is projected from movements (no setter anywhere) — a liability, not a figure. Proven in `tests/integration/loyalty-points.test.ts` and `tests/integration/stored-value.test.ts` |
| `services/orders` — API-07 | ~150 lines | **Built and persisting.** Promise and reserve in one call; short lines told before payment. On-hand comes from the **inventory service's own projection**, deliberately not a second one — the function that promises stock to a customer must not drift from the one that counts it |
| `services/fulfilment` — API-08 | ~160 lines | **Built and persisting.** Every attempt recorded; proof never deleted; cash settled per driver per run — and now attempts against orders **not on the run** are reported too. The dispatch list itself is not on this surface yet (M20) |
| `services/migration` — API-12 | ~120 lines | **Built.** Refuses a production target on every request; serves the verification report |
| `services/ai` — API-13 | ~170 lines | **Built.** Proposals only; kill switch needs no approval; budget checked before the call |
| `services/` remaining domain services | **none** | **All thirteen APIs are served.** API-01…13 each have a service; see the surface gate below |
| `services/api` — composition root | ~220 lines | **Built.** Config checked at boot, store opened, fourteen service modules (thirteen APIs — `pricing` is a second module under API-02) on one router **built exactly once**, drains on SIGTERM. **All persist.** Tokens are verified against the configured identity provider; `services/api/src/roles.ts` holds the role catalogue, because a role is configuration and its holders are tenant data |
| `infra/` | compose (db · migrate · **api** · web), Dockerfile, CI deploy job | **Built.** One command brings the whole stack up on one machine; CI builds the image, proves it refuses a bad configuration, and brings the stack to ready. The database password the deploy step generates is **URL-safe** (hex, never base64 — a `/` in the userinfo makes `DATABASE_URL` an invalid URL and migrate exits 1), guarded by `tests/guardrails/the-db-password-is-url-safe.test.ts` |

**The consequence, stated plainly so it cannot be missed (reconciled 7 Aug 2026 against the audit):**
the business rules are finished and the system is **only partially assembled**. An earlier version of
this paragraph said "there is no service layer" — that is now **stale**: a thin service layer exists
and all thirteen APIs persist to PostgreSQL (see the table above). But two facts qualify every
"Built" below and must be read with it:

1. **The services are thin.** Six of the seven domain services (`finance`, `orders`, `inventory`,
   `customer`, `fulfilment`, `reporting`) are kernel-only re-implementations that do **not** import
   the rich, tested domain engines in `packages/`; `services/purchase` reusing `packages/purchasing`
   is the lone deliberate exception. **35 of 77 packages are imported only by their own tests** —
   built and unit-proved, but absent from anything that runs.
2. **"Built" ≠ end-to-end.** A requirement row reading *Built* means its rules are implemented and
   unit-proved. It does **not** mean a cashier can scan an item today, nor that the row is
   *WIRED / INTEGRATION TESTED / E2E VERIFIED* in the sense of the completion vocabulary below.

The ASSEMBLY / WIRING workstream (below) tracks each module through that vocabulary. This section is
the honest counterpart to the family table above and must be read beside it.

## Completion status vocabulary (adopted 7 August 2026)

Per owner instruction, "engine implemented and unit-tested" is **not** "module complete." Every module
is now tracked through this ladder; a module is COMPLETE only when the whole chain — requirement →
domain engine → application/service → API/event contract → persistence → authorization → UI/channel
where applicable → integration tests → E2E → operational evidence — is connected and verified.

`NOT STARTED` · `ENGINE ONLY` · `PARTIALLY WIRED` · `WIRED` · `INTEGRATION TESTED` · `E2E VERIFIED` ·
`UAT VERIFIED` · `PRODUCTION VERIFIED` · `EXTERNALLY BLOCKED`

Never report `ENGINE ONLY`, simulated, synthetic, mocked, or documented work as product completion.
The evidence each rung requires is the mandatory **`docs/MODULE-COMPLETION-TEMPLATE.md`**, copied into
every module-assembly PR; a module moves up only to the highest rung whose boxes are ticked. This is
**CI-enforced**: `tests/guardrails/completion-ladder-has-evidence.test.ts` fails the build if any
module below claims `WIRED`+ without registered evidence that exists (and `INTEGRATION TESTED`+ must
point at a real integration/e2e test), so an engine-only module cannot be labelled complete. The
shared E2E harness (`tests/support/api-harness.ts`) and local/test IdP (`tests/support/local-idp.ts`)
are how `E2E VERIFIED` is proved — through the real surface, real token verifier and real per-tenant
authorization, the way `main()` composes them.

The audit found five defined extension requirements with **no traceability row** — a silent-drop that
the roadmap forbids. Restored here with honest status; none is invented, all trace to the requirement
text cited.
